1695. ELIZABETH PINK, ELIZABETH LLOYD , and HANNAH CARROLL were indicted for stealing, in the dwelling-house of Hannah Carroll, 6 10 l. Bank-notes, the property of Thomas Jackson.
MR. BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS JACKSON . I carry on no business. I live at Ramsgate—on the 22nd June, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, I went to No. 4, Francis-street, Vinegar-ground, Old-street, and inquired for the prisoner Pink, who I had known before—I had seen her twice before in that house—I was told she was not at home, but was over the way—she came to roe shortly after, partly undressed—when I went into the house I had nine 10 l. Bank of England notes wrapped up in brown paper, in my right band breeches pocket—I had seen them a quarter of hour before I entered the house, and am quite sure they were in my pocket—I had a gold watch ana chain, and some gold and silver in a purse in my left hand trowsers pocket—I wasshown into the parlour, which is a bed-room—the prisoner Carroll who is the landlady, was there—I went up stairs to a bed-room, as Carroll said she had another woman, and saw Lloyd in bed—I came down again directly I afterwards went to bed with Pink in the parlour—I folded my trowsers up and put them behind the pillow, and slept nearly three hours—when I awoke
I found my trowsers upon a chair on the other side of the bed, and the prisoners were all in the room drinking wine—I got out of bed, searched my trowsers, and missed six of the 10 l. bank notes—the brown paper was there, containing only three notes—I have not recovered any of them—one has been paid into the Bank since—Pink gave me back my watch and chain, which was also gone, and also my purse—the notes had not been in the purse nor in the same pocket—I had been up all night before, and had met some philanthropic friends—I swear I had the notes, because I unfolded the paper in the street about a quarter of an hour before—I did not count them, but am quite satisfied I had my whole number—I am satisfied I had more than three—in the course of the afternoon, Lloyd and Pink were taken into custody—I stopped the notes at the Bank the same morning—I hart received them from the Bank.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You had known Pink some time T A. I had seen her twice—I never lost anything in her company before—I have been in her company with money and with my watch and chain—she induced me to take some spirits—she was in bed with me when I went to sleep—he has occasionally put my chain round her neck, and returned it to me—I have given her money to do certain things with, and found it all right.
COURT. Q. You trusted no notes to the prisoner? A. No—I had received ten notes—I only paid one away, and I saw that one afterwards—I did not make a mistake about the number of one of the notes at the Bank—I had paid one away—the clerk said, "you had better go back and get the number o(that note"—I said it was either the first or last of the roll, because in opening the packet of notes I should take either the top or bottom one—I went to the person I paid it to, saw it, and it was the very first of the ten—I then returned to the Bank—I traced one note to the Bank last Tuesday or Wednesday—the other five have not been heard of—I have paid away the other three to the party J paid the first to—he gave me cash for them—I did it on purpose that one person might have the four.
MR. HORRY. Q. Do you recollect any of the placet you went to on that night t A. I peeped in at the Caledonian coffee-house, but did not sit down there—I had been in the neighbourhood of Hackney—I was in a public-house in the neighbourhood of Brunswick-street for some hours—I might have been a little the worse for what I had before I left there—I was going to Vinegar-ground to see Pink—I had a lodging where I could have left my money, but being late I did not like to disturb my landlord—it was certainly not at four o'clock in the morning, instead of eight that I went to the Vinegar-ground—I did not go to sleep down stairs before Pink came—I did not go to bed before she came—she induced me to send for some drink—I was not in the house a quarter of an hour before Pink came—I was with Carroll principally—she went over for Pink—she was not gone two minutes—the moment Pink came we went to bed together, but I never intended to have gone to bed at that early hour of the morning—I only just went there to see her—my purse was in my pocket with some gold—I lost about seven sovereigns—Pink returned me two of them—she handed me my watch and chain and purse—I had put my watch on the side-table over night—my purse was in my pocket folded up-with my trowsers—I did not put my chain round her neck when I got up in the morning—she was up and dressed—it was between ten and eleven o'clock—my watch-glass was broken, through their taking it out of the house—I will swear I was not toying with her, talking about not having a ring to fit her, and wanting the landlady to get a cab to get a license to marry her—I was Perfectly sober when I awoke, but was greatly excited—I had been on London-bridge smoking a cigar before I went there—I did not drink.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where did you get all the money?
A. In payment for a check for 120 l. for the sale of some property near Brighton—I do not know the name of the street, or How long I have had the property.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460817-1696]
1696. JANE GILL and JOHN JONES were indicted for stealing 1 cash-box, value 2 s.; 3 pair of sugartongs, 245.; 1 child's coral, 16 s.; 3 necklaces, 10 l.; 14 rings, 6 l. 10.; 1 butter-knife, 12.; 2 veils, 11 s.; 1 pair of spectacles and case, 1 s.; 10 sovereigns, 10 half-sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, and 2 shillings; the property of Elizabeth Barkley, in her dwelling-house; and THOMAS WEBB was indicted as an accessary to the said robbery.
MR. BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH BARKLEY . I am a widow, and keep a house of accommodation in Rupert-street, Whitechapel—the prisoners Gill and Webb lodged in my house for about a fortnight—they left a fortnight before the robbery—I desired them to leave—on the 17th Gill came to my house—she said she had met a friend from the Indies, and she was coming that evening to stay—she came about ten o'clock in the evening with the prisoner Jones—we were in the kitchen —they fetched some gin and half-and-half—they went out again and came back j in about an hour—it was before twelve o'clock—(when Gill came she said she was coming with a friend from India, and told me not to tell Thomas Webb, as she did not wish him to know)—when they returned I fetched some brandy—Gill and Jones made some brandy and water—one made it and the other sweetened it—I drank half a glass of it, and did not like it—the rest wai 1 chucked away—my servant Woodward drank some of it—Gill and Jones went out of the room to go to bed, and took a candle and candlestick with them—their bed-room was on the second floor—I had told Gill the room I she was to go to—she knew all the rooms in the house—it was not the I room she had occupied when she lived there, but she particularly wanted I me to save her the best room—I found afterwards that no room had I been occupied—after I drank the brandy and water I fell asleep—at that tine I the outer door of the house was open—it is not usual to go to sleep and leave I the door open—my servant was in the room—I awoke about six o'clock in I the morning, before the servant—I shook her and awoke her—I then went to I my bed-room, which is on the same floor as the kitchen, to go and lay down I—I found the room disordered, and a great hole in the bed like the mark of a I cash-box—I examined my drawers, missed my cash-box, and 15 l. 10. in I gold, and two lace veils—I had seen the things safe the same evening, the 17th—the cash-box had been locked in a drawer, which had been opened as I if unlocked—the veils laid on the box—the box contained a number of trinkets and jewellery—I found the candlestick which Gill had taken up to go to I bed, in my bed-room—I am sure it was the same—the candle was put out—I there was a small piece of candle left in it—this is the candlestick—the candle I was four or five inches long when they had it—there was a very small piece I left in the morning—it might have burned about an hour—I went up to the second floor room, and found nobody had been in it whatever—nothing was disordered—I am confident no one had slept there—some one slept in the firstfloor—they are trades folk, a gentleman and lady who always come there—they were there till one o'clock in the day—no other bed in the house had been slept in—I know this pair of spectacles by a mark adjoining the hinge, and I know the case—I have had them a long time—they were left in my care—I did not use them—I saw them safe on the 16th, the Thursday before I was robbed, on u small press bedstead in my kitchen—Jones had not been in my hosse before to my knowledge—I know this cash-box and ear-rings to be mine—the ear-rings and the rest of the things were in the box the day before-1 certain Jones is the man who was there that evening.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long have you kept the house? A. Not exactly thirty years, but I have been in the neighbourhood Gill had nothing to drink with me when she called in the early part of the day—I took some brandy-and-water with her in the evening—I did not have any gin and half-and-half—it might be eleven o'clock when they came the second time—they stopped some time in the kitchen with me—there are a number of candlesticks in the house—I found the outer door shut on the single lock in the morning—I always double lock it—the kitchen is on a level with the outer door, and with my bed-room, and with the stair-case—it never happened before that any one went into my bed-room—there is no sofa in the second floor room—my bed did not appear to have been used, except by putting the cash-box in it—I had no drink that evening but what I had with them—this is the only candlestick of the kind that I have—the others are tall large brass ones—persons who come to my house sometimes bring the candlestick down stairs with them, but nobody brings them to my bed-room—it is usual to leave the candle outside on the stairs—I am not in the habit of using glasses—I never used these spectacles at all—they do not suit my eyes—they were left in my care by a gentleman ten months ago—he never came for them—I do not know whether they would suit the sight of a young person—Jones was taken on the Saturday after the robbery—I never mentioned the loss of the spectacles before that—I never knew they were gone till they wereshown me—I know them because one of the glasses is cracked, and by the old case—I know it by the mark on it—this piece of leather has been torn off since I saw them last—it hung down ragged, but was not torn off—I nevernoticed any stamp on it—it was a case as old as this—my servan drank a drop of the brandy-and-water and did not like it, she chucked the rest away—she only tasted it—I found her fast asleep at six o'clock in the morning—Ishook her to awake her—she had not been drinking before that I know of—it was very difficult to awake her—she had not brought in anything to drink—I do not know How many couple were in my house that evening—there were a very few—they do not come without my knowing it—I receive themoney from all the parties—there is a fastening to my bed-room—it was shut that evening with the key in the lock.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Had any person who came that night been in your bed-room? A. No, I am confident of that—they could not go there without my knowing it, unless I was asleep—I was not asleep before, that evening—there are not two entrances to the bed-room—the entrance is in the passage, it is not necessary to go into the kitchen to go to the bed-room.
SUSANNAH WOODWARD . I am servant to Mrs. Barkley—on the 17th of July, about ten o'clock in the evening, or a few minutes before, Gill and Jones came to the house together—Gill said to me, what will you have—I said nothing—she said, "Have something"—I said, "If I have something you must fetch it"—she fetched spirits and some half-and-half, which we drank together—we all four partook of it—they went out together and returned in about half an hour—about eleven o'clock they said, "What will you have to drink"—somebody said brandy—and Mrs. Barkley herself fetched half a pint,2 s. worth, from the public-house—it was mixed by Gill and Jones—a person called me to the street-door, and when I came back mistress had a glass by the side of her—she drank some of it and said How sweet it was—Gill gave me a glass in the kitchen—I said, "Jane, what have you put in this brandy"—she said, "Nothing"—I swallowed a good mouth full of it, and chucked the rest away"—I was sitting in the chair and went fast to sleep—I did not see them go out to go to bed—Mistress awoke me about six o'clock, saying "Susan, I am robbed"—! suid, "Who has robbed you"—she said " Bulley, Jane"—my
mistress usually sleeps in the back room, on the same floor as the kitchen—it opens into the passage—when I went to sleep the outer door was open—I found it shut in the morning—mistress opened it herself—I saw this candlestick found in my mistress's bed-room.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You did not see what candlesticks they took 1 A. No—this candlestick was on the kitchen-table with a candle in it before I went to sleep—we have only this one candlestick of the kind in the house, the others are very large brass ones—we have another Ten large tin one which is very seldom used—my mistress took as much half-and-half and gin as we all did—that did not make us tipsy—I drank a good mouthful of the brandy-and-water—I threw a good drop of it into the coal. hole—I dare say a gill—it was bitter and sweet, both—it was very nasty-there was no brandy-and-water left in the morning—I did not see what my mistress drank—I never saw my mistress with these spectacles, but can swear to the other things—I did not let in so many as half a dozen people that evening.
ELIZABETH BARKLEY re-examined. When I awoke in the morning, the brandy-and-water that I had left was chucked away—I did not chuck it away.
MARY ANN MARTIN . I am an unfortunate girl, and live in Rupert-street. On Friday night, the 17th of July, about a quarter to ten o'clock, I wasatthe Garrick tavern, and saw the three prisoners and another man drinking—they all four crossed the road, and went into a public-house opposite—I went there and saw them—I heard Webb say to Gill, "Will you haveany more drink"—she said, "No"—I heard her say, Where shall I meet you"—I did not hear the answer—I saw Gill and Jones together again about a quarter to two o'clock that night, going up Christopher-court, Rupert-street, near Mrs. Barkley's Howe—Jones had something square carrying by the side of him, wrapped up in something dark, with one corner hanging down—they were going out of Rupert-street, going in a direction from Mrs. Barkley's house, and not above three minutes' walk from it—they were both together—I was standing at Mrs. Smith's door, No. 1, Christopher-court—there was a gas-light opposite—Mrs. Smith goes by the name of Bowner—I have no doubt of the prisoners Jones and Gill being the parties—Jones had a black band round his hat, very broad, and I knew Gill before.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BBIBN. Q. Had you seen Jones before that evening? A. Yes—they went risjht before me up the court, as I stood at the door—I am living with Mrs. Barkley now—I was not then—after I was examined I went to live there, and am there still.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where is the Garrick tavern. A. Ie Leman-street, Whitechapel—Cox's is opposite—when Jones and Gill went away and said "Good night, "Webb and the other man remained at the public-house—I had seen Jones three weeks before, coming out of the London docks—he told me he was a foreman in the docks—he did not speak te me when I saw him in the public-house.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Were you ever taken up on uy charge? A. No.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Is it unusual for a girl in your situation to live in one house more than another? A. No, I do not often move about, but my landlady was in the hospital, and I staid there till she was better.
SUSANNAH WOODWARD re-examined. I did not notice Jones' hat that night.
ELIZABETH BOWNER . I live in Christopher-court, Rupert-street. Ontw 17th of July, about a quarter before two o'clock, I saw a man dressed in dark
clothes come by the door, and saw Gill, with a man dressed in dark clothes, come oat of Mrs. Barkley's house—she drew the door to after her—I saw it was shut to—I cannot say it was fastened—they came out together—I saw nothing with them—their backs were towards me—I do not know the man—I saw Gill's face—I had seen her before, and known her by sight very well for a long time.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How far were you from them? A. Within one house of them—they came out of No. 1, Rupert-street—I was opposite No. 3—I followed them because they were going the same way as I was—they went up the court, and I lived up the court—I went into my own door, and saw no more of them—they were in the court when I entered it.
CHARLES DORKMAN . I live in Tollington-street, Whitechapel-road. On Friday night, the 17th of July, between twelve and one o'clock, I saw Webb, just by Whitechapel-pump, going towards Rupert-street, about two minutes' walk from it—I knew him—he asked me to have a pint of beer, and I refused—he and Gill lived at my mother's at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not he say, "Holloa, Charley, How are you?" A. Yes, and asked me to have half a pint of beer.
AMELIA SCARLETT . I am single, and have been staying some time with Mrs. Dorkman—I knew the prisoners Webb and Gill. On Saturday, the 18th of July, between four and five o'clock in the morning, I saw them at the Blue Anchor public-house, Whitechapel-road, drinking half-and-half—that is not more than a quarter of a mile from Rupert-street—I left them there—I had been staying at Dorkman's about a fortnight.
ELIZABETH DORKMAN . I live in Thomas-street, and let apartments—tha prisoners Webb and Gill lived three weeks with me, as man and wife. On Saturday morning, the 18th of July, about five o'clock, they both came boms together.
HENRY BOARD . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Lipman-street, Cambridge-road. I found this cash-box in my cow-yard, which is three or four minutes' walk from Thomas-street—I do not know How far from Rupert-street—my yard is in the high road—the box was open—there was an ear-drop in it.
THOMAS KELLY ( police-constable H 119.) On the day of the robbery I apprehended the prisoners Webb and Gill, at Mrs. Dorkman's house—I told them they were charged with stealing money, but I did not know the particulars at the time—Webb said he knew nothing about it, he was quite ready to go—Gill said, "Is that the charge?"—I said I could not exactly tell her the charge, she would know it at the station—I received the cash-box from the inspector, and the ear-drops—I apprehended Jones on Saturday week, walking in the Whitechapel-road—I told him the charge—he said I must be mistaken, he knew nothing about it—I searched him at the station, and found a sovereign, 5 s. 6 d. in silver, three farthings, and these spectacles and case—he requested to have the spectacles back, but I would not give them to him—he refused to tell me where he lived—he said twice that the spectacles were of no use to me, and wanted them back, but Mrs. Barkley was asked whether she had lost any spectacles—she said she had, and if they were hers the right sight was broken—she said that before she saw them—I had not seen them myself—she said the right side was cracked—I know Board's cow-yard—it is three quarters of a mile from Rupert-street, and three or four minutes' walk from 1 hornas-street—beyond Thomas-street, coming from Rupert-street.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. When did you see Mrs. Barkley after the robbery? A. Not till the prisoners were at the station—I heard nothing then about any spectacles, not until they were taken from Jones—Mrs. Barkley was asked if she had lost a pair—she said she had a pair, and if
she had lost them, if they were hers, the right sight was cracked—I apprehended Jones about a quarter of a mile from Rupert-street, at half-past eleven at night.
MR. O'BRIEN, on behalf of Jones and Gill, called
JAMES BOOTH . I am a working-jeweller, and live with my employer, Afo, Sumner, in Church-row, Houndsditch. I remember repairing a pair of spectacles for the prisoner Jones.
COURT. Q. Have you been in Court during this trial? A. Only about two minutes—I do not know who brought me in—he is somebody's clerk, I believe—he has something to do with this case—he brought me in to show me the spectacles—I do not know How he knew who to apply to—that is the person—( pointing him out)—he showed them to me, and asked if I could identify my own work—I said, "Yes,"and after seeing them I went out of Court—my attention was called to part of the bridge of the spectacles, which has I been broken—one of the glass places had been broken off—I recollect mending them, because I can tell my own workmanship—Mr. Jones, the prisoner's brother, I believe is the person who found me out—he subpœnaed me—I do not see him in Court—Mrs. Sumner carries on the jewellery trade—she is not here.
MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Look at these spectacles—( looking at them.) A. I believe I can safely say I mended these—it must be eight months ago—I know them because I generally mend them with brass solder—here is the J mark—I had not mended other spectacles for the prisoner, that I am aware I of—I charged him 6 d. for these—he paid it to Mrs. Sumner—I did not I know him before, but I recollect his countenance—Mrs. Sumner is not here. I
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. You live where you are employed? A. Yes, I am a journeyman, and live in the house—I swear that—some persons use silver solder to mend steel spectacles—I have not mended any but I with brass solder, since it has been found to be better—I have mended spectacles for three or four years—I cannot say How many pairs I have mended I in Mrs. Summer's employment.
JURY. Q. How is it this spot is not blue? A. You cannot blue that—I we do not blue them all over—the glasses were not broken when I mended I them, that I am aware of.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Do you mean, by looking at this piece of solder, tony I it is your own work and no one else's? A. I can tell my own workmanship.
Q. Has not this mark been made in the same way—( handing him another pair)? A. I believe it has—it is not my work—it is mended neater thin 1 I should do it—nobody else mends with brass solder—I never heard of it—I I mended these—I do not know that the prisoner left any name with them—I they were repaired while he waited—it was in the winter and was about the I middle of the day—Mrs. Sumner was present in the shop, no one else—I mentioned to her that I was coming here—I called her attention to what I was coming for—it is seldom I repair spectacles.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Did you ever see the prisoner before that time, on your oath? A. No—I remember the last pair I repaired there—Jones's brother first came to me about this—I did not know him before—he said called to know if I recollected mending a pair of spectacles some time ago for a young man who waited for them—I asked How long it was since—he said, "About eight months,"and then I called to mind that I did do a pair—I have not had any money from the prisoner's brother or from the attorney in this case—he told me his brother was in trouble, and told me I was wanted if I could identify the spectacles I repaired for the young man—he told mew was charged with stealing them—he said he wanted me to identify the person
and the spectacles—he did not describe the man who had left them, he only uid he was a tallish man—he did not tell me How he was dressed—I did not look at the dock when I came in here to see the spectacles—I did not look where the prisoner is standing—I had not seen him from the time I mended the spectacles till I saw him here—I had not seen him in custody—this is the eighth day I have been waiting here—I cannot say why I did not look at the bar—I have turned my head and seen him several times since I have been here—I did not look at his face when I came in, because I knew I should know the man—I did not give it a thought to look at him when I came—I cannot exactly say it is eight months since I repaired the spectacles—I will swear it is a month—I did not make an entry in the books of the job.
FRANCIS EVANS . I am waiter at the King and Queen public-house, Mile-end-road. I know the prisoner Jones—he is iu the habit of wearing blue steel spectacles.
COURT. Q. Was there anything remarkable about them? A. The right glass was broken—he wore them in that state—I have not been in Court—I was applied to to prove this last week—I think the right glass was cracked across the centre, perpendicularly—he frequently wore them in reading.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Tell us any time you have seen him wearing blue steel spectacles with a crack? A. Several times—the last time was four or five mcmths ago—I am a waiter—I worked in the docks before that—1 have not been in trouble—the prisoner did not work in the docks to my knowledge—Idid not know him at the docks—I do not know How he gets his livelihood—I do not know him more than seeing him at my master's house—I was brought here last Wednesday—the friends of the prisoner came to me.
COURT. Q. Did he come into the public-house wearing the spectacles) or take them out of his pocket and put them on? A. I have seen him take them out of his pocket, take them out of a black case, I think, and put them on—it wai not a new case—I cannot swear to the case—I can to the spectacles—these are the spectacles—( looking at them)—I know them by this crack—I have bad them in my hand four or five months ago in the public-house—he did not take them off and hand them to me—they were lying on the table when he was done reading the paper—I took them up and said, "Are these yours?"and gave them to him—I do not know whether he put them into his pocket.
COURT to ELIZABETH BARKLEY. Q. Take the spectacles in your hand—had you noticed any join about one of the eyes? A. It is here—I had tried them on to see if they suited me, and they did not—I merely noticed the way the glass was broken—I did not notice the little join and the brass—I bad them, to the best of my knowledge, abput ten months—they were left at my house by accident—I had not seen Jones, to my knowledge, before the night he came with Gill—he could not be the person who left them—the case is the same, except a piece has been torn off—I know the spectacles by the broken glass.
SAMUEL TAYLOR . I am street-keeper of Whitechapel—the prosecutrix's house is in the parish of St. Mary, Whitechapel.
GILL — GUILTY . Aged 28.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 30.
Transported for Ten Years.
WEBB — NOT GUILTY .

1698. SAMUEL WILES was indicted for stealing 2 s., the monies of William James Harris, his master.
WILLIAM JAMES HARRIS . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Charles-street, Back-road, St. George in the East—the prisoner was in my service—in consequence of suspicion, on the 13th of August, I marked five shillings, and put them into my till about five or six o'clock—I went about seven o'clock, and found them safe there—I then marked two more shillings and a sixpence, and put them in the till—about eight o'clock I went to tie till, and missed one of the shillings—I then took the sixpence out, and left the six marked shillings in the till—I sent the prisoner over to the ware-house—he came back, and I went into the parlour at the back of the shop—I watched, and saw two children come in for half a pound of butter—they gave him a shilling, and he gave them sixpennyworth of halfpence in return, which he took from the till—there was plenty of copper in the till, but do silver but what was marked—I directly went into the shop, and there was bat five shillings in the till—there ought to have been seven—I took the prisoner by the collar and said, "What have you done with the two shillings you have just taken out of the till?"—he trembled very much—I pushed him into the parlour—he said he knew nothing about it—I told him I certainly should send for a policeman—he then said he would give it me back if I would for give him—I said, "I told you I was robbed a few weeks ago by the last boy, and you knew Tery well what I intended to do with the next I caught"—k said, "Pray forgive me on account of my mother"—I gave him into custody, and two shillings were shaken out of his boot—one was marked—the other was not—this is one I had marked with a scratch on the top of the crown.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How many shillings have you marked 1 A. I never marked any before—I did not state before the Magistrate that I marked six shillings—I marked five shillings first, and two shillings and a sixpence afterwards—this is my signature to this deposition—I did not say I marked six shillings, and put them into the till, where there was no other but copper money—this was read over to me, but not about the six shillings—I was watching the prisoner all day—I had a good opportunity of seeing what be did.
COURT. Q. are you sure you marked five shillings at five o'clock? between five and six o'clock—about eight I found the five shillings right—i then put in two shillings and a sixpence more marked—i sent the prisoner to the warehouse, and about nine o'clock the children came, and gave him a id-ling—shortly after I missed two shillings, and two shillings were found in in boot—one was marked, the other was not.
ROBERT BOARD ( police-constable K 232.) I was called on the night of the 13th of Aug. to take the prisoner—I told him Mr. Harris charged him with robbing him—he begged him to forgive him for the sake of his mother—I found two shillings, five pence, and a farthing, in his trowsers pocket, and in his boot two shillings, one marked, and the other not—he begged Mr. Harris to forgive him.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460817-1699]
1699. AUGUSTUS PHIPPS was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 21. 4 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 14 s.; 1 waistcoat, 9; 2 handkerchiefs, 5 and 3 half-crowns; the property of Henry Joseph Bligh; and SAMUEL WHICHELOW , for feloniously receiving 1 coat, 1 waistcoat, and 1 pair of trowsers, part of the same, knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. EVANS conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY JOSEPH BLIGH . I live in Dorset-street, Marylebone—I am some-times known by the name of Harry Messenger—I work for Mr. Messenger, a greengrocer. On Saturday night, the 25th of July, I went to my brother's, in Compton-street—I was walking, about nine o'clock that night, in Compton-court, with Amelia Knox—we met the prisoners and George Tibbey at Leicester-square—we all went together towards my residence—when we got to South-street, Phipps said, "There is my aunt"—I said, "Where?"—I looked round, but could not see her, and Phipps was then out of my sight—Whichelow then said, "We will have some porter"—we went into a public-house, and staid there about ten minutes—I then went borne—Whichelow wanted me to stop, but I would not—he, Tibbey, and Knox, went home with me, and I found the area grating open—I said, "Oh, my God 1 the place has been robbed"—I went down to the kitchen, where I live—the things were very much displaced, and I could not stand, I was so giddy—I do not know what made me so—I did not miss anything on that occasion, but about two o'clock on Sunday, the next day, I missed my coat, waistcoat, a pair of trowsers, and 7 s. 6 d., which had been in my waistcoat pocket—I saw the prisoners that day, and told them I had been robbed—they said, "Oh, have yoa? who do you suspect"—I said, "I don't know who to suspect"—Whichelow said, "Do you think it is Dudley Moore?"—I said, "I don't know"—he said, "We will go and see"—Phipps and Whichelow went to see—I went to the station and gave information—Phipps came to me, and said Dudley Moore had plenty of money, and he had not been at work that day—I went with the policeman, and took both the prisoners—in coming along Oxford-street, Phipps said, "I will get you the clothes back for 8.; they are sold at Mrs. Bateman's, and I will give you 10 s. to say nothing about it"—I said, "It is not in my hands now, it is in the constable's; I cannot do anything with it"—just before we got to the station, Phipps said, "Will you—now or never?"—I knew both the prisoners—Whichelow was in my employ—I had some goats and chaises—he used to go out with them.
Cross-examined by MR. NATLOE. Q. Do not people call you Messenger always 1 A. No—some people do, because I have been at work so long for Messenger—I was robbed about five years ago—I do not often have tricks played with me—I have seen Phipps's aunt—I could not see her that night—I was not tipsy—we had one pot of porter—I then went to Dorset-street—I did not go to bed—I was lying by the side of the bed when I awoke, about half-past seven o'clock the next morning—I did not suspect Dudley Moore of stealing my things before—he is not a friend of mine—I know him very well—Phipps said the things were sold at Mrs. Bateman's—she is not here—she was at Marylebone office—Phipps said he could get my clothes back for 8.—he did not say he could get them of Tibbey—the prisonersere on intimate terms with me—we never went out and walked together.
COURT. Q. You found the area grate open? A. Yes—I had left home about half-past seven o'clock—it was then shut down close—I went back about twenty minutes before eleven o'clock.
THOMAS SKINNER ( police-constable D 102.) On Saturday, the 25th of July, I saw the prisoners walking together, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, in Mortimer-street, Portland-place—Whichelow had a bundle under his arm—I saw Knox and Tibbey about thirty yards behind the prisoners—I followed them, and lost sight of them at the bottom of Mortimer-street—I took both the prisoners on Monday morning—I took Phipps first—he said
he did not know anything about it—as we were going along Oxford-street with the prosecutor, I heard Phipps say he would give him 8. to get the clothes back, and 10 s. on the Saturday night to say nothing about it—the prosecutor said, "you did not care what you did to me, and I don't care what I do to you; it is in the officer's hands"—just before we got to the station door, I heard Phipps say, "Now is your time, or never"—Whichelow said, "1 shall speak the truth."
Cross-examined. Q. Did not Phipps say, "Now is your time, or never, to get your clothes back from Tibbey A. I did not hear that—he might have said it—I am quite sure he said he would give the prosecutor 8 s. to get the clothes back.
MR. EVANS. Q. Tell me the precise words you overheard Phipps make use of? A. He said he would give him 85. to get the clothes back, and, on the Saturday night if he would say nothing about it—it might be that he would get the clothes back.
GEORGE TIBBEY . On Saturday night, the 25th of July, I was with the prisoners, the prosecutor, and Knox—Phipps left in South-street—he said, "There is my aunt"—I did not see his aunt—the rest of us went in a public-house—we stopped ten minutes—we then went towards the prosecutor's—we left him next door to his residence—in about ten minutes I saw Phipps with a bundle—he said it was the prosecutor's clothes—I did not hear anything then about selling them—we waited about ten minutes in Dorset-street to see if the prosecutor would come out again, which he had promised, but he did not—we then went on towards Hopkins's-street—Whichelow carried the bundle—he was with Phipps, and I was about twenty yards behind with Knox—I saw Whichelow at a door-way in Hopkins's-street—we all went on, and Whichelow came after us and asked if 8 s. would do for the clothes—Phipps said yes, as he could not take them home—Whichelow went back and brought the money—he gave it to Phipps—he said he had 6.—I did not I see it—Phipps 'gave back to Whichelow two half-crowns and a sixpence—I then I received half-a-crown from Whichelow.
Cross-examined. Q. What had you the half-crown for? A. My share—I did not assist in stealing them—I knew they were to be stolen some time I before—I was bound to come here to-day—I came to forward the ends of I justice, not to save myself—I did not steal bread and candles—I did not I work at a candle-maker's—I know Knox very well—she gets her living by I washing—nothing else that I am aware of—she lives with her mother—she I never lived with me
AMELIA KNOX . I live in Pulteney-court. I remember Saturday night, I the 25th of July—I was with the prosecutor and Tibbey in South-street—we I went into a public-house and came out about ten o'clock—I afterwards saw I Phipps in Dorset-street with a bundle—he said they were the prosecaton I clothes—he took them a little away, and then gave them to Whichelow—they went to Hopkins-street—I saw Whichelow stand at a door-way in Hop—I kins-street—Phipps stood at the next door—Tibbey and I walked on—I Phipps came soon after, and Whichelow came and said would 8 s. do—Phipps I said, "Yes"—W'hichelow went back and brought 6.—that was divided I between the three—Bligh is sometimes called Harry Messenger—about iff I weeks ago I heard Whichelow say he intended to get the prosecutors clothes.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go on to Pulteney-court? A. Yes—I there—Tibbey went to see me home—he did not go in with me—he I fancy man"—I do not support him—I do not know whether I do everything I else for him—I did not know that these things were to be stolen, only what
Whichelow said to Tibbey—we only staid in a public-house two of three minutes while a pot of beer was drank—Tibbey did not have the clothe—he bad half-a-crown of the money.
PHIPPS — GUILTY . Aged 17.

1700. MARY COSTER was indicted for stealing 5 shirts, value 2 l.; 1 blanket, 2 s.; 1 coat, 10.; 1 scarf, 2.; and 1 fork, 6 d.; the goods of Charles Butcher, her master.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
CAROLINE BUTCHER . I am the wife of Charles Butcher, of Cheynes-street, Tottenham Court-road. The prisoner was in my employ about two months—the last time was about six weeks ago—I employed her as a char-woman, to light the fire and clean the place—the last time she was there I missed a sovereign—shortly before her leaving I missed two shirts—I dis-charged my other servant because I thought she was dishonest—the next time I saw the prisoner after she left, I went to ask her to come to cook a supper—she was in liquor, and her husband would not let her come—she ran away from her husband—on the 15th of Aug. I saw her—she said, "Mrs. Butcher, How do you do?"—I said, "Very well, you are the person I want"—she first seized me by the neck, she then let go her hold and ran up stairs—I followed her, and sent for a policeman, and gave her into custody-next day I searched her premises with the policeman—I found a shirt on her husband's back, marked with my husband's name—I found a coat hanging up which I had not missed, and a black scarf in a band-box, a fork, and a blanket on the bed—they are my husband's—I had not given the prisoner any of these things—this is the coat—these are my articlet.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. This is an old coat? A. Yes—my husband worked about in the house in it—I know the shirt by the name on it—it is old, but it is of use—I was married at Northampton—I have not been married twice—I have been married to Mr. Butcher between three and four years—I have never been separated from him—I had not employed any person but the prisoner as a charwoman—I gave her the gown she has on now, and a bed-quilt—I have sold furniture to Mr. King, a broker, with my husband's permission—I believe the prisoner lives with Mr. King—my husband has not been in the habit of selling things—I have not given anything away within six months—I gave her three blankets to wash, which came home—this shirt is the worst that was found.
MR. PARNELL. Q. At the time this shirt was missed other shirtawere missed? A. Yes, four—they were good ones—this is an old one—my husband was not before the Magistrate—the prisoner stated that I gave her the things—if Mr. Butcher had sold either clothes or furniture to Mr. King I should have known of it.
' MR. MELLOR. Q. There was some quarrel between you and the prisoner? A. Yes—I went to her house—she has not been living with her husband ever since she left me—I went after her several times—the door was always locked—shewas away for six weeks from her lodging.
WILLIAM WILLIS ( police constable E 64.) I took the prisoner—I went with Mr. Butcher the next morning to the prisoner's lodgings, and found these articles.
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.— Confined Three Months.

1702. THOMAS CHURCH and JOHN BROWN were indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 1 l., the goods of Sarah Smith.
SARAH SMITH . I am a widow, and live at Leytonstone. On the 11th of July I left my watch in the parlour about one or two o'clock—I was absent from the room about five minutes—when I came back the watch was gone—the window was open—this is my watch.
WILLIAM FRAZER . On the 11th of July, between one and two o'clock, I saw the two prisoners near the chapel pump, at Leytonstone—I saw Brown come out of Mrs. Smith's gate—Church was on the other side of the road—he could see Mrs. Smith's house—Brown took something out of his pocket, and showed it to Church, and then they went away—I described the boys.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Had Brown any poultry with him? A. No—he had no basket—you go through a gate to the house.
SAMUEL BUTLER BAKER . I am an engineer. I received information of this loss, and a description of the boys from Frazer—I went across the forest, and saw the prisoners—I followed them a short distance—I went and found this watch in a spot near to where I saw the prisoners.
RICHARD LAMB . I saw the two prisoners together after this loss—they were sitting down at a corner near where the watch was found—I saw then about an hour and a half after the robbery, and the watch was found within two hours.
CHURCH — GUILTY . Aged 17.
BROWN — GUILTY . Aged 17.

1704. HARRIET GRIFFIN was indicted for stealing 4lbs. weight of pork, value 2 s., the goods of John Barnes.
JOHN BARNES . I live at Stratford, in Essex, and sell pork. On Saturday, the 18th of July, I saw the prisoner inside my shop about nine o'clock at night—she inquired the price of pork—she looked out a piece, as I supposed, with the intention of having it weighed—she did not buy anything, to my knowledge—I made a motion to my lad to look after her—she left the shop, and in consequence of information I sent for a policeman—she no brought back to my house, and the policeman produced the pork—I knew it by some discolouration on the top of the rind—it was the outside piece, tw had got stained—I told the prisoner she had got into a bad mess, and w offered to pay me for it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was the weight ofrt— A. I do not know exactly—it was worth more than 2 s.—I have known w prisoner two years by coming to my shop—I frequently suspected her has bought things, and paid for them—there were a good many customers—this was the piece of pork the prisoner was inquiring about.
HENRY LAMBERT . I am in the prosecutor's service. The prisoner came to the shop and asked the price of a piece of pork—I showed her a piece, and told her the price was 6 1/2 d. a pound, and I would weigh it outside—she was inside the shop—I told her to bring it out—she brought it out, and walked away with it—I made some inquiry of my master—I went, but could not see her—I got a policeman, and went to Forrest-gate, where she lives—the policeman went in, and came out with her—I saw the pork—it was the same piece I hadshown her—she was brought back, and she said she would pay my roaster for it.
JOSEPH BENTON ( police constable K 381.) I went after the prisoner to her house—her daughter sent for her—she was not at home—I found this piece of pork.
GEOROIANA UPTON . I was assisting Mr. Barnes that night—I did not take the money for this pork.
NOT GUILTY .

1705. JOHN LARKINS and ANTHONY BARRETT were indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 10., the goods of James Pincott Cornwall Reynolds.

Larkins. It was not me took it. Witness. I am quite sure of his person.
MARY WOODFORD . I saw the prisoners going over the gate—I am quite sore they are the two persons.
Larkins Defence. A person was standing at Mr. Reynolds's gate; the bell was broken; he asked me to get over, which I did; the coat was near the door, I did not touch it; it was Charley Evans took it.

1706. JAMES LANGFORD was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM BANKS . I live in New-row, Deptford, and deal in fire-wood—the prisoner was in my service—it was his duty to make bundles of wood, and to go out with it occasionally, and receive money for the wood when be sold it—on the 17th of July I sent him out with wood—his own boy went out with him, with a horse and cart—the boy came back with the horse and cart—the prisoner did not return—he was taken on the 31of July—taought to have returned on the evening of the 17th of July, and if he had received any money he was to pay it the same night, when he came home—he was to sell the wood by the hundred—if he got more than the price I charged he was to keep that to himself—the wood I sent him with that day was worth 1 l. 16 s. 9 1/2 d.—I produce a note which he sent to me when he was taken—some man brought it to me—I do not know the writing.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not lend me the horse and cart? A. No.
EDWARD JONES . I am in Mr. Banks' employ. On Friday the 17th of I went out with the prisoner—he had Mr. Banks's horse and cart, with
fourteen hundred of wood—we went to sell it—I sold seven hundred of it, and he sold seven hundred—I saw him receive some of the money, not all—I saw him receive 5 s. 4 d., 5 s. 6 d., and 2 s. 4 d.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( police-constable R 202.) I took the prisoner on the evening of the 30th of July—I said, "Langford you must go to the station with me"—he said, "What about?"—I said, "Respecting a load of wood of Mr. Banks's"—he said, "That is all settled"—I said, "I have not understood that, you must go with me."
Prisoner's Defence. He lent me the horse and cart, and trusted me with the wood; I had the misfortune to lose half the money, and I could not make it up; I sent the letter and said I would come back and pay him 6 s. a week till I had paid it.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Confined Four Months.

1707. FREDERICK WARD was indicted for stealing 3 pairs of trowsers, value 3 l. 10 s.; and 2 1/2 yards of woollen cloth, 1 l. 2.; the goods of George Tabret Lock, and another; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE TABRET LOCK . I am a tailor, and live in Woolwich—I had some trowsers in my shop on the 22nd of July—I missed them—I went I with Samuel Watts on board a Waterman steam-packet at a little after one o'clock—I went into the cabin and saw the prisoner—he had two paper parcels—Watts opened them—they contained these three pairs of trowaen, which I can swear to as mine—this pair is my apprentice's make, they are all the property of me and my son, who is partner with me—this piece of cloth I cannot swear to, but a piece was missing from my shop, which was brought in the night before for a customer.
Prisoner. Q. What can you swear to the trowsers by? A. This pair I can swear to by taking in three-eighths of an inch at the bottom; and these I black ones, this was a remnant of kerseymere, and they are my making—I they are all my making, or the apprentice's.
ROBERT MORRISON . I am apprentice to Mr. Lock—about one o'clock on 1 the 22nd of July I came up to my dinner and saw the prisoner in the shop—I I asked if he wanted to see Mr. Lock, he said, "No, I have seen him"—I did I not know whether he had seen him or not—I left him there.
SAMUEL WATTS . I am parish constable of Woolwich—I went on board I the Waterman steam-packet, which was coming to London, between one I and two o'clock on the 22nd of July, and in the cabin, I saw the prisoner I tying up a paper parcel—he had another parcel with him—I found in one I parcel these two pairs of trowsers, and in the other this piece of cloth, and I these other trowsers—I asked whose they were—he said his—I asked where I he got them—he said he bought them—I called the prosecutor, he identified I them.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) I produce a certificate of the I prisoner's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted 24 th Nov. 1845, and confined three months)—the prisoner is the person.

[trialAccount t18460817-1708]
1708. WILLIAM PITCHER and HERBERT GRAHAM were indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 watch-guard, 5.; 1 watch-chain,35.; and 2 seals, 2.; the goods of George Small, from his person.
GEORGE SMALL . I am a wheelwright, and live in Southampton-mews, Euston-square. About two o'clock in the day, on the 4th of Aug., I was at Woolwich—I had had a pint of stout on board the vessel I went down in—was a little in liquor—I recollect going into a public-house—I do not know How long I was there—I think I must have gone in about three o'clock—I had
about seven half-crowns in my pocket, a chain round my neck, and a watch in my fob, and two seals—I am sure they were safe when I went into the public-house—the prisoner Pitcher came in after me—I do not remember anything more till I found myself at the station-house—this is my watch, chain, and seals.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know when, where, or How you lost your watch, chain, and seals? A. No—they ought to havt been in my pocket—I went to the Duke on Horseback, they tell me, but I never was there before—I went down by a steam-boat.
MARY DAWSON . I am single, and live in Rope-yard-rails, Woolwich—I was in the Duke on Horseback on the evening of the 4th of Aug.—I saw the prosecutor and the two prisoners—they all came in together—a pot of beer was called for, and the two prisoners shoved the prosecutor against the fire-place—they came away from him directly, and his right-hand pocket was tamed inside out—Pitcher then went back again to him and took bis watch-guard off his neck, and his watch out of his pocket and put into his own—the two prisoners were then going out, and they said to another marine who wat there," Come on"—he said,"Indeed I won't"—the prisoners went out, and I never saw them again till they were at the station—the prosecutor stopped till Mr. Harris came and fetched him.
Cross-examined. Q. How came you to be there? A. I went to have a pint of beer, the same as anybody else might—both the prisoners put the prosecutor to the fire-place—it was Pitcher took the watch—they were all drunk—the pot-boy was there, but he did not see what I did—I beard Pitcher tell the Magistrate that the pot-boy said when he went out, "Well, Pitcher, I hope you will remember an old friend when you come back"—the pot-boy was examined—he is not here.
JOHN MARSH . I am waiter at the Duke on Horseback. On Tuesday, the 4th of Aug., I saw the prosecutor there—Pitcher and two or three more were there—I do not recollect seeing Graham—I went into the tap-room when a pot of beer was called for by the prosecutor—I took it in, and was waiting for the money—Pitcher said the prosecutor would pay me, and if he did not he would—I went out for another party, and when I returned I found the prosecutor on his back, on the floor against the door—Pitcher was coming out of the door—I said to him, " Bill, don't go away, pay me for the pot of beer; you said you would"—he said, "I will pay you for the pot of beer, Jack "and he gave me 4 d. worth of halfpence—when I went in with the pot of beer the prosecutor had some seals hanging down in front of him, and a chain round his neck—when I went in with a pint of beer afterwards I missed the chain and seals—I told my master.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you let them all go before you went for the policeman? A. Yes—the prosecutor appeared to me to be mad—he did not draw out his watch, and smash it on the table in my presence.
THOMAS WILLIAM GILLING . I am shopman to a pawnbroker at Greenwich. On the 4th of Aug., about a quarter past seven o'clock in the evening, Graham pawned this watch—there was a chain to it—I gave him the chain back, as it was of no value—about five minutes after I went to the door, and saw Graham go and join Pitcher, and they went away together—Graham told me he had had a fight, and the watch had got damaged, and it was his own.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you often take such things of drunken people? A. He was not drunk—he knew what he was about—I cannot say he was sober—the glass of this watch is broken, and it is battered.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) I went to the Duke on Horse-back on the 4th of Aug.—I saw the prosecutor—he was tipsy, and dancing
about the tap-room like a madman, and complaining he had lost his wath—I took him to the station, and at twelve o'clock that night I-took the prisoner! together in a lodging-house in Hog-lane, Woolwich—I told them it was for taking a man's watch—they said, "We have got no watch; you may take us, if you like."
WILLIAM GLADMAN ( police-constable R 122.) I assisted in taking Pitcher from the station to the cell—Graham was there at the time—Pitcher said to me, "Bob, as it has come to this, I will tell you where we threw the seals and chain"—Graham came up and said, "Yes, you will find them at the back of the Eagle, we threw them between two trees"—I went and found them there—these are them.
(Graham received a good character.)
PITCHER — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
GRAHAM — GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Eight Months.

1710. JULIA O'LEARY was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; 1 pepper-box, 2 d.; and 4 drinking-glasses, 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Elwood, her master.
SARAH ELWOOD . I am the wife of Thomas Elwood—we keep the Fountain, in the Broadway, Deptford—the prisoner lived in my service—she came to me in Feb. and left on the 18th of April—while she was with me I missed a pepper-box, a handkerchief, and four glasses—I discharged the prisoner in consequence—this is the pepper-box and the glasses—they are my husband's—this handkerchief is mine.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You once had the prisoner and her sister in your service? A. Yes—this pepper-box has our private mark on the bottom—we were in business, and sold these sort of things—I have one more of these boxes—this one has been in use twelve months—I misted this on the 18th of April—I had seen it within a month before that, in the kitchen—this is a common handkerchief—I cannot say to a day when I had seen it, but it was within two months—I had had it about three months—these are ordinary drinking-glasses—one of them has been ground down—I am sure about this one.
COURT. Q. Is there any mark on the handkerchief? A. No, but in locking my drawer I shut one corner of the handkerchief in, and it is cut—here is where it was cut.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) I went to a house in Mill-lane—I found this pepper-box and two glasses—I apprehended the prisoner at the same house—I told her she was charged with stealing a quantity of glasses, and other property of Mrs. Elwood—she said she knew nothing about them—she said she bought the glasses and pepper-box, and Mrs. Elwood gave her the handkerchief long ago—Mrs. Elwood denied it.
Cross-examined. Q. She speaks Irish very strongly, does she not?. A. Yes—she did not say that they were Mrs. Allen's, and Mrs. Allen bought them.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18460817-1714]
1714. MERCY WILD and EMILY LAWSON were indicted for obtaining 2 l., from Ann Sonnex, by false pretences.
RICHARD THOMAS HART . I am a carpenter, and live at the Duke on Horseback, High-street, Woolwich. On Tuesday, the 7th of July, between three and four o'clock, I saw the prisoner Lawson there—she asked me whether Mrs. Sonnex was at home—I said she was engaged—she said she came from Mr. Edwards, for the loan of 21.—Mrs. Sonnex is my sister—I went into
the parlour and spoke to her, and she refused to give her the 21.—Lawson came again, and brought the note produced—I read it and took it to Mrs. Sonnex—she told me to take 21. off the tea-board—I took a sovereign and two half-sovereigns—they were my sister's—I wrapped them in a piece of tissue-paper, and gave it to Lawson—I know Mr. Edwards, and so does my sister—I gave Lawson the 21. in consequence of the note—I did not know either of the prisoners before.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you tell Lawson she must bring a note? A. Yes, I sent her away for it—I will swear I believed it to come from Mr. Edwards—the word "friend" is spelt "freind"—I will swear I was taken in by the note—Mr. Edwards is an engineer in the dock-yard—I thought this note came from the water-works.
CLARISSA MATILDA MORNKE . I keep a Berlin-wool shop, at Thomas-street, Woolwich. On the 7th of July, about three o'clock, the prisoners came together to my shop—Wild said, "Will you oblige me with pen, ink, and paper, as I have lost two notes, and do not like to go back for a third"—she asked me to write for her—I wrote the note produced—Wild told me what to write—I then said, "It is to be signed"—she said, "Yes, 'J. Edwards" 1—I wrote "J. Edwards"—I inquired if it was to be sealed—she said it was of no consequence—I gave it to Wild and she gave it to Lawson—I had never seen Lawson before—Wild had been a customer of mine—she said she wai tired, and told Lawson to take the note—she went away with it, and Wild remained at my shop about twenty minutes—Lawson came back and said, "Mrs. Sonnex will send it; here is your half-crown," and gave her half-crown—she had other money in her hand—I did not notice what it wai—Wild had purchased goods in my shop, and paid me 3 s. 6 d. for them—I bad to give her 2 d. change, which she refused to take—she tied some money in the corner of her handkerchief, and they went away together.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Have you seen Wild often? A. Yes, more than once—she is a cripple—I was not surprised at her being tired and sitting down—I never heard anything against her character—she said,'I have lost two notes—Lawson was there at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not Wild, in addition to what you have told us, say, "Be careful, and do not lose the note?" A. Yes.
JAMES EDWARDS . I am a smith, and live at Woolwich, and supply the town with water—I know the two prisoners—I never authorized them to go to Mrs. Sonnex, to get money for me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Your name is not James only? A. No, James Thomas Edwards.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable.) On the 11th of July I took both the prisoners into custody—I took Wild first—I told her she was charged with obtaining 21. from Mrs. Sonnex—she said Lawson told her to go and do it—Lawson was not present—I took Lawson—she said Wild tempted her to do it, or she would never have done such a thing.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
WILD — GUILTY
LAWSON — GUILTY
Recommended to mercy by Prosecutor and Jury.
Confined Nine Days, and delivered to their friends, who engaged to employ them.
SURREY CASES.
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.

1715. JOHN WICKS was indicted for embezzlement.
SARAH WATTS . I am the wife of Thomas Watts, of the Yorkshire Grey public-house, Park-street, Islington—I ordered some coals of the prisoner,—I cannot say whether I was present on 13th Jan. when an account was paid—I paid this bill of 41. 6 s. on 14th April to the prisoner, and he wrote this receipt, also this bill of 3 l. 8 s., on 15th May—both these receipt were signed by the prisoner in my presence.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You did not order the coals of Mr. Pegg? A. No, of the prisoner—I knew nothing of Mr. Pegg in the transaction on either of the occasions—I have dealt with the prisoner since—he is now in the service of Mr. Wright.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you take the first quantity of coals on his own application? A. Yes, to my husband—I do not recollect hearing it made—I thought I was dealing with him—the bills are in Mr. Pegg's name—I thought it was on commission.
NATHANIEL PEOG . I am a coal-merchant at Phœnix-wharf, Southwark—the prisoner has been in my employ several years—it was his duty to deliver bills and to collect money—he did that in his capacity as my servant—he had to keep a petty cash book—this is it—it was his duty to enter in this book any sums of money he received on my account—he was to pay them over at the end of the day, and balance the gross receipts at the end of the week—the book was the cheque I had ou-him in balancing—I paid him 30. a week—he had no claim for commission—he was in the habit of making a charge for beer and other disbursements—they are in this petty weekly disbursement book—at the end of the week I looked at the disbursements, teeing the sums he received, and he accounted to me—I know his handwriting—I have looked at this bill of 13th Jan. 1846—the receipt is in bis handwriting—here is entered in the petty cash-book—"On 13th Jan. 21. 6 s. as received from Watts"—the bill was 2 l. 10 s.—in another petty cash-book here is entered, "On 14th April, Watts, Park-street, 4 l."—the bill is 4 l. 6 s.—he has not accounted for anything in the cash-book on 15th of May—it appears, by the cash book, that there was a settlement at the end of these respective weeks—the book is balanced—here is the entry of 13th Jan.—there are disbursements that week—they are carried out of the little petty disburse-ment book into the gross amount at the end of the week—that is all settled, according to the entries in the book—the 15th of May is the last time he was in ray employ—he also kept a wharf-book—the clerk could not make out the bill without the price—he would have to inquire of the prisoner the price of the coals if it was not entered—when he put the price it should be the correct price at which they were sold—there is a price in this instance—the entry in Jan. states, two tons at 23 A. —there is the same entry in the petty cash-book—that is not the price at which the bill is made out—the clerk ould make out the bill according to the price in the book—by holding the book up, it plainly appears in the book that he has written one price over the original price, in which the darker ink predominates.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When before the Magistrate you had not discovered any supposed alteration in the book? A. No, I think I discovered it the very day I obtained a warrant against him for a previous case about three weeks after he left—he left on the 16th of May—I applied or the warrant on the 13th of June—there was more than one examination—he was held to bail—I did not discover the erasure till all the examinations
were over—I forget whether I discovered it after the final examination or not—he has been in my service twelve years—he did not give me notice that unless I raised his wages 6 s. a week he would leave—I gave him notice because I found frequent drunkenness and neglect of duty—he said after? giving him notice that he could not stay unless I gave him 1 s. a day more—he certainly did not give me notice—I remonstrated with him in consequence of his being absent some hours—he assumed a very high manner, as if I could not do without him, and on that we parted—just at the end of the time, he anticipated I was going to take him back, and said he would not stay without 6 s. a week more—Mr. Moore, my clerk, is allowed to sell coali for his own profit—that is the only instance, for I deprecate the system—I do not know from him that the prisoner had the same privilege—Moore it allowed to draw coals for his friends at a less price than I charge my other customers—he has done so ever since he entered my employ—he has been with me three or four years—he is charged a profit of 5 s. upon the pool price—Wickens is a customer of mine—he is adealer in coals, and had them at 5 s. on the pool price.
Q. Suppose the prisoner, right or wrong, took coals for sale to his friends at the same price as Moore, would these entries be right? A. No, the pool price on the 13th of Jan. was 18 s. 6 d. a ton—the price charged in the book is 4 s. 6 d. extra, 23 s., but the price charged in the bill it 25 s.—that would make a difference of 6 s. 6 d.—the entry at the trade price would be right, all except 6 d. t if he was allowing 4 s. 6 d. it would be right—he does not account for the last entry in the cash-book—the pool price here was 16 s. then—4 s. 6 d. added to that, would make 20 s. 6 d.—three tons would be 3 l. 6 d.—I had not quarrelled with him before he left—Mr. Moore was constantly seeing the books—I have the ledger here—the entries are Moore's writing—after the prisoner left went to Mr. Nash's public-house—I do not know that the prisoner lodged there—there was a removal of some of his goods—my wife had given his wife some things—she was anxious that they should not appear in a public-bome among his wardrobe, and I went to Mr. Nash's—the prisoner called afterwards, and asked what I meant by overhauling his things—I parted with him under the best of feelings—this might be a fortnight or ten days after he left—he had improved my business—I considered him an excellent servant-after he left some of my customers told me he had solicited them for a new master—he went into the service of Mr. Wright, who took a wharf 500 or 600 yards from mine.
Q. Did Wright commence business there the very day you took the prisoner up? A. I cannot recollect, but as soon as I became acquainted with those facts I went to my solicitor—a great many of my customers have gone to Wright—some of them are now returning—they were hucksters who came to the wharf for their coals—it was his usual business to treat with thent, and receive their money—I know nothing of Watts—he had not been a customer of mine—I very likely saw Mr. Wright at the Coal exchange on the 21ft of May—I do not recollect saying to him, "I see you are going to be a neighbour of mine, and take my late clerk into your service"—I will not swear did not—he did not say, "Yes, is there anything against his honesty?"made no reference to character, nor did I say, "Not the slightest"—he mip have said, "Have you anything to say against him?" and I studiously avoided giving any answer.
Q. Did not you say "Not the slightest?" A. Certainly not—it is impossible for a man to recollect everything he says—I cannot recollect whether I did or not—it was after that I discovered this—the prisoner's brother called on me on a Saturday.
Q. Did not you say to him, "I should not have cared where he had gone, if he had not gone to that white-livered son of a b----, Wright?" A. I did not, it is not language I should have used—he came to beg his brother off—I said nothing of the sort.
Q. Did you say, "He will get half my connection, keep him till he gets the remainder, and then turn Wicks about his business?" A. I recollect nothing of the kind—I cannot swear it did not pass, but I think it very unlikely—I forget whether I saw hhn on the 6th of July.
Q. Did you say to him at the Southwark police-court," I suppose I shall hear again of what I said about your brother and Wright?" A. No, I do not recollect seeing the brother there—I certainly did not say so to him—I will not swear he was not there—I will not undertake to swear whether I said so or not
COURT. Q. Was it Moore's privilege to be charged 5 s. profit, taking the larger profit himself f A. Yes—the prisoner in this instance would have rather a less profit.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is there an account opened for Wicks in your ledger? A. Yes—my clerk opened it for his more immediate guidance—this entry is in Moore's writing in the ledger—the amount entered to Wicks on the 13th of Jane is exactly the same as the prisoner has accounted for in the cash-book—I had daily opportunities of seeing these entries.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When the prisoner came into your employ, did he claim the advantage of selling coals? A. He did not—I should not have allowed it—till I got to the Police-court I had no notion of any such claim—he did not receive money from the customers generally, only from the hucksters—I have no opportunity of knowing what customers he introduced—when Moore came to me I agreed to allow him this privilege, as he had several connexions in the docks—the prisoner entered his disbursements in a book—I gave him into custody simply on discovering these defalcations—when I had the conversation with Mr. Wright I had not made the discovery—the entries in the ledger were made from his book—I have never said that he was honest since I found out this—the ledger is in Moore's writing—if there was an error in the prisoner's book it would go through the whole of the books.
WILLIAM MOORE . I am clerk to Mr. Pegg. On the 15th of May I received 3 l. from the prisoner, on account of Mr. Watts—it was just as he was about leaving—I made out the whole of these bills—I had a conversation with the prisoner in one instance as to the price to be charged in one of these bills—I think it was the 2 l. 10 s. in Jan.—I know it was one of these tiiree—he came into the counting-house and asked me to make out the bill for the amount in the journal not to appear the same as the bill—no one but ourselves were present—he told me the amount to put in the bill, as it is made out 25 s.—I made no observation to him—I made out the bill at 25 s. per ton, leaving of course the journal the same—I did not make any alteration in the journal—I made it out accurately, without reference to the journal 1 had the journal before me—I had let the amount in the journal appear less—I understood he was to charge the customer one price, and account for another—there have been some bills made out before for his immediate friends, 1 believe, where he would have charged the customer more than he paid himself—I was aware of jt—as a fellow clerk I thought as they were his friends he might get 1 s. or 2 s. by them—I had no directions from Mr. Pegg to do so—whatever he chose to charge his own friends was no matter of mine—I did not communicate it to Mr. Pegg—I cannot remember whether the fair and proper charge was paid to the employer—there is a much lower price—it
might just have remunerated at the trade price—the price in the bill was not raised to give him a premium above the proper price, but reduced so as to gire him the benefit—I have allowed very few of these transactions.
Q. Look at this entry to Mr. Watts in this page, did you look at it when you made out the bill? A. No—I posted it from here into the journal—it was decidedly not in this state at the time I posted it—I made it in the ledger as I found it—if it had been 23 s., as it is here, I should have entered it so—it first went to the sundries account—the account was not opened then—I cannot say whether there has been anything under this figure—I posted this entry—( looking at one)—the prisoner told me the price—I originally had 26 s.—it was the price he told me when he asked me to make the bill out—it was merely to make it for 25 s., which I did, and at that time he told me to make an alteration here in the book—I cannot remember when it was—I cannot find the lesser amount—the entry is correct—it is 2 l. 6 s.—the price I is added since I posted it—the next entry is 23 s. to another party—I do not know anything about any erasure—here is 23 s. put to the entry—it was not here at. first—it is Wicks' entry.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You have made out many bills for Wicks, at larger prices for his friends? A. Yes—those accounts were entered in the ledger—I entered the short price in some few instances—it is impossible to say How many, perhaps half a dozen—there is an account of his extending over more than twelve months—that is not kept the same as my account—the parties to whom the coals were sent is entered in Wicks's account—he is made debtor to his master in the ledger, but is different to mine—in some instances the party who bad the coals is stated in the margin—that is after I certain time—in the earlier part of his account the parties are not named that is thesame as my account—the accounts are balanced from July, 1843, to the present time—Mr. Pegg could refer to these accounts at any time.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. There is a debtor and creditor account to the prisoner in the ledger; what is the reason of that? A. My first inducement to put his name was, there was a dispute with him about an order that came through some friend in some way which remained unpaid some time—the prisoner disputed as to having any knowledge of it—this account enables us to refer to parties he recommended—there is no other object in it—I had no authority to make any abatement to him—he never said he was entitled to abatement—he asked me to alter the bills as a matter of favour—Watts's name does not appear in the prisoner's account—if I do not receive an order myself I ask the prisoner what price he sold coals at—I enter the price he tells meii the journal—he made no claim to have them at a lower price—this 23 s. the wharf-book must have been put there since I copied the entry into tbc journal—if he was entitled to the deduction there would be no object is making the alteration—no such arrangement existed with the prisoner.
COURT. Q. How do you enter when you are allowed 5 s. profit by your master? A. The price I am to pay myself, not that which is charged to the customer.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You had a right to charge the customer anything you pleased? A. Certainly.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know of any dealings with Watts except the three items named? A. They are the only transactions.
MR. CLARKSON to MR. PEGG. Q. Did you say to the prisoner, about the time Moore was married, that Moore was indebted to you 57 l.; that he was going to be married with your money, and you should close the account both with Moore and himself? A. Certainly not, nor anything like it.
COURT to NATHANIEL MOORE. Q. Do your observations respecting putting
down a different sum to what was received on the prisoner's application, apply to the three entries? A. Only to one, and I think it was the first.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460817-1716]
1716. JOHN WICKS was again indicted for stealing 2 sovereigns and 2 half-sovereigns, the monies of Nathaniel Pegg.
JOHN WARE . I am a wheelwright, living in Glasshouse-yard, Aldersgate-street. In April last I ordered fourteen tons of coals of the prisoner—the-price was to be 22 s. 6 d.—I am not at all acquainted with the prisoner—they were to be sent on the following Thursday or Friday—they came with these vender's tickets, which are dated 17th April—I gave the order direct to the prisoner at the prosecutor's wharf—the check produced was given for the fourteen tons of coals—it is drawn by my father, and it is made out to Messrs. Pegg and Co.—it was given to Alfred James, who is in Mr. Pegg's service—this bill was delivered previously; I do not know who by—I think the boy brought it the day before, and said if the money was paid the following day five per cent would be allowed—the amount is 15 l. 15 s.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is the date of the check? A. The 16th of May—before the transaction we had a van for sale—we had had no dealings with Mr. Pegg at that time, and knew nothing about him—the prisoner came with a man named Wickens, who purchased the van—I should think that was a year and a half before I bought these coals—I cannot recollect whether I asked 50 l. for the van.
Q. Did you afterwards agree to take 43 l. or 42 l., and to give Wickens an order for ten tons of coals? A. I gave him an order for ten tons, which I received from Mr. Pegg—Wickens was the retail dealer, I believe—from that time I ordered coals at the same place—the bill for those coals was delivered to Wickens, not to us—I ordered the coals in question at Mr. Pegg's counting-house—I never had any dealings with Pegg till the dealing about the van.
Mr. BALLANTINE. Q. With whom did you deal for these coals? A. Mr. Pegg—I knew nobody else.
ALFRED JAMES . I am clerk to Mr. Pegg. I called on Mr. Ware with a hill, 15 l. 15 s., for these coals, by the prisoner's desire—he told me if Mr. Ware would give me a check I was to take off five per cent, discount—I left the bill on the Thurday, and got the check on Saturday—I changed it, and received 2 5 l. notes, and the rest in silver—I gave the whole to the prisoner—in the course of the day he gave me 5 s., for my trouble in getting the money—I have collected for him on several occasions—he once before paid me for collecting from the same party—that was the only transaction he paid me for, to the best of my belief—I showed the check to the prisoner, and got the money for it, by his desire.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had Moore the check in his hand some time before you got the money? A. Not that I am aware of—he was in the office—I do not believe he had it—he could see the transaction—he wag not present when the prisoner sent me for the account, but he was when I showed the prisoner the check, not at the time he told me to get it changed that was five or six minutes after—I think I can swear he was not present he saw the check—I cannot swear he did not have it for an hour—he knew I was going first, and knew I had been the day before—the bill was made on one of Mr. Pegg's invoices—( looking at the ledger)—there is the name of Ware in this account—I never heard anything about the van—I collected one or two hills for clover for the prisoner—I received nothing for that—the 5 s. was for the whole—Moore knew I had the 5 s.
COURT. Q. Did Moore know the whole of the transaction? A. Yei, I believe so—he knew I went for the account, and that I got the check—I believe he was present when I was sent to get it changed, but I am not certain—he did not appear to make any entry of the transaction at the time—I told him afterwards that I had received the 5 s.—it might take me an hour and a half or three quarters to collect the money for the clover.
WILLIAM MOORE . I remember nothing of this transaction of the check, of the money received for it—the whole was not paid over to my master—I never saw the check.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you prepared to swear you never saw that check? A. I am—the boy told me, a day or two after the transaction, tint he had had the 5 s.—I have been present during his examination—I swear distinctly I never had the check in my hands—I never heard the prisoner tell him to get it cashed—I knew he was in the habit of receiving profit on a few customers whom he introduced—I had nothing to do with it—he made bills out himself unknown to me—I may have been a party to six or sera such transactions—it is impossible to say it is not a dozen—it has extended over a long time—I have no idea How many—Mr. Ware's name appears in our books, certainly not in the ledger, it is in the journal—these coals are charged in the ledger to Wickens—he is a retailer—we never carted any for him but once—we charge him so much—I did not go to the public-home, and have a jolly drink over this transaction—I will swear that.
COURT. Q. Are we to understand that in this transaction Wickens's naoe appeared as the purchaser? A. Yes—we trusted Wickens, not Ware—we allowed Wickens to make what he could above 5s, on the pool price.
NOT GUILTY .
