1717. ELLEN FINNIGAN and JOHANNA GRIFFIN were indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin; to which
FINNIGAN pleaded GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLARK conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH EMERY . I am the wife of Mr. Emery, a publican, of Gravel-lane, Southwark. On Wednesday, the 8th of July, the prisoner Finnigan came for half a pint of porter, which came to a penny—she gave me a sixpence—I gave her 5 d. in copper, and put the sixpence into the till—there was a half-crown there—she went out of the shop and a policeman came in immediately—I took the sixpence out of the till, and gave it to him—he told me to mark it—I saw nothing of the other prisoner.
JOHN HENRY HOLLAND . I am shopman to Mr. Wither's, of Blackfriars-road. The prisoner Finnigan came to my master's shop for half an ounce of tea and I quarter of a pound of sugar on the 8th of July—I served her—it came to 2 3/4 d —she gave me a bad sixpence—I gave her the change, and she left the shop—I had the sixpence in my hand when the policeman came in—it tad not been out of my hand—I marked it, and gave it to him.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman.) On the 8th of July I was in company will Whitlam, in plain clothes, in High-street, in the Borough. I saw both tin prisoners in company, walking and talking together—I followed them, fist into the Ram public-house, Borough-market—I saw them come out again and followed them to another public-house—Finnigan went into Mr. Emery's the other prisoner stood about twenty yards past the door—I remained outsit I until Finnigan came out—I then went into the house, and received a I I from Mrs. Emery—I came into the street, and saw both the prisoners on the curb-stone, about thirty yards from the house, round the corner—they
were handing something from one to the other—I saw the hands go from one to the other—I saw nothing pass—they then got up, and went to Withers'a shop in the Blackfriars-road—as they passed the door I saw the hand of one pass to the other, at the door—Griffin handed something to Finnigan; then Finnigan went into the shop—Griffin remained round the corner—Finnigan came out, and joined her—I followed them into the Westminster-road, to another public-bouse, and took them—I found 13 d., in copper on Finnigan, and 13 1/2 d. in copper, and a 4 d. piece, was found on Griffin—I followed them from seven till nine o'clock—they were both together the whole time
JOHN WHITLAM ( policeman.) I was with West on the 8th of July, and saw Finnigan come out of Withers's shop—I went in, and received a sixpence from Holland—Finnigan was searched, and a good shilling and a penny found on her, with some tea and sugar.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These sixpences are both counterfeit, and both cast in the same mould.
JOHN WHITLAM re-examined. There was no place where Finnigan could have got rid of the copper—she never spoke to any other person.
GRIFFIN — GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460817-1718]
1718. JOHN SMITH and EDWARD BROWN were indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLAKKSON conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL COULBER . I keep the Ship and Mermaid public-house, Snow's-fields, Bennondsey. On Sunday, the 28th of June, about half-past tea o'clock in the morning, my house was closed—I heard a knock at the door, and on opening it the two prisoners came in—I asked what they wanted—they said they were travellers, and wanted refreshment—I said, "What I" they said, "Half-a-quartern of gin"—they came to the bar and I served them—it came to 2 d.—they gave me half-a-crown—I gave them 2 s. 4 s.—I did not notice that the half-crown was bad till five minutes after they wcrt gone—I had put it into my pocket—I had no other money there—I then concealed it in my bed-room, in a pewter sugarbason on the sideboard—nobody but my son was in the house at the time they came in—he did not see what I did with the half-crown—it remained in the bason till the following Sunday—I never told anybody of its being in the bason—oo Sunday, the 5th of July, about ten o'clock, the two prisoners again knocked at my side door—I was watching for them, expecting them—I looked through the window, saw they were the same men, and let them in—they asked for half-a-quartern of gin and laid down half-a-crown—I took it up, drew the gin, and said, "This is a bad half-crown; you gave me one, you smashers and coiners, this day week"—they wanted me to give it them back—I kept it, called for a constable, and bolted both my doors—before the constable came the prisoner Brown said he would knock my by head off if I did not give him the bad half-crown, and he would give me a good one for it—I refused—shortly afterwards a constable came in, and he sent for another—when he came I gave the prisoners in charge, and said I had taken another bad half-crown of them that day week—they did not deny having been there—one of them, I do not know which, said, "It was not a bad half-crown I gave you, it was a bad shilling"—I sent my son for the half-crown, and told him where to find it—I give them both to the policemen—they were both marked in my pretence.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you speak to anybody about the first half-crown? A. No, I kept it in my pocket about half an hour—I did not let anybody else into the house that morning untill one o'clock—I had not many customers that afternoon—I had not seen the prisoners before—I
am certain they were the same parties that came on the next Sunday—they did not say "It was not a bad half-crown no more than a bad shilling" they called me bad names—Smith threw down the half-crown the first time, Brown the second—it was before one o'clock on the 28th that I put the half-crown into the bason, before I opened my house.
GEORGE SAMUEL COULBER . On the 5th of July my father called me to the bar to fetch a bad half-crown out of a sugar-bason on the sideboard, in hi bed-room—I found it there, brought it down, and gave it to him.
WILLIAM RANDLE ( policeman.) On Sunday, the 5th of July, I wasieot for to Mr. Coulber's house, and found the prisoners there—I took them—another constable was sent for—I found 2 s. 4 d. in good money on Brown—I received these two half-crowns from Coulber, which I produce—they were marked in my presence—I heard Smith say he gave Coulberi shilling the Sunday before—Coulber had said it was a half-crown.
ROBERT WOOD ( policeman.) I was called into Mr. Coulber's—I searched Smith and found two shillings, two 4 d. pieces, and 1 1/2 d. on him—Coulber said, "I give these men in custody, they came here this day week and gave me a bad half-crown"—Smith said "It was not a bad half-crown) it wasabad shilling"—I am sure those were his words.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin—these half-crowns are both counterfeit, and both cast in the same mould.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 27.
BROWN — GUILTY . Aged 25.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460817-1719]
1719. JOHN WILSON, MARY ANN JONES , and JAMES LLOYD were indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
MARY LEARY . I am a fruit-seller, and live in New-court, White-street, Southwark. On Friday evening, the 10th of July, between nine and ten o'clock, I was selling fruit in the Dover-road, and saw the prisoner Wilson and another man, who I believe to be Lloyd—Wilson wore a cap, and the other a hat-Wilson said, "What have you got there, old lady?"—I said, "Gooseberries"—he said, "Will you give me a pennyworth?" which I did, and he gave me a shilling—I said, "Is that a good shilling?"—he said, "Oh yes, it is"—I said, "If it is not, why do youk'give it to an old woman like me?"—Lloyd wu standing by the stall near him at the time—I gave them lie?, and they went away—I put the shilling between my teeth, took it out, and had it in my hand when West, the policeman, came up—I gave it him, and he took it away.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not you say at first that Wilson was not the man? A. No—I am sure of that—I was asked if I knew the man—I looked at him, and said, "Why, he wore a cap"—I swore to bin when he had a cap on—I did not when he had a hat on—he wore a cap at the stall—when I saw him again he had nothing on—the policeman put a cap of him, and I said, "That is the man"—the shilling had not been out of hand till the policeman had it.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman). On the 10th July, about nine o'clock into evening, I was in company with Whitlam, at Newington Causeway, andsif the three prisoners walking and talking together—we followed them—we saw them near St. George's Church—I lost sight of them for a minute, and thea saw Lloyd and Jones standing at the corner of the Church, about ten yards from Leary's stall, in view of it, and Wilson at the stall—I crossed over on the same side of the way, and saw Lloyd pass and repass, and stand about two yards from the stall—when Wilson left the stall he followed him—I went up to the stall, and Leary gave me a shilling, which I produce—I then went towards wards
the Town-hall, in the Borough, and saw the three prisoners walking in company—this was about four minutes after I left the stall—I saw them James and Lloyd came back again, and Wilson was waiting ten or fifteen yards from a stall in High-street, but the cabs came in my way, and I lost sight of him—I saw Jones and Lloyd go towards the stall—I did not see them arrive at it—I kept my eye on Wilson—I did not take the prisoners in charge till next night.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not Wilson fifteen yards from the first stall? A. At first—Jones and Lloyd might be ten or fiteen yards, I cannot say which—I was about ten yards off, on the opposite side of the way—Lloyd and Jones were looking towards the stall—Lloyd ran by the stall, returned, and stood about two yards from it, with his hands in his waistcoat pocket—I saw them together afterwards, but did not see them join company—I did not see Wilson near the second stall, nor the others at it—they went towards it—I lost sight of Wilson while they were going towards it.
MARY BRIAN . I keep a fruit-stall, and live in Maypole-alley, Southwark. On the evening of 10th July, the prisoner Jones cametomeandasked for a halfpennyworth of gooseberries, which I gave her—shegave me a shilling, and I gave her elevenpence halfpenny change—Lloyd, I believe, came up while that was going on, and asked me if I had any gooseberries—I told him no, the woman had bought the last I had—Jones said to him, "I will sell you my lot for one penny"—Lloyd said, "you want to gain profit"—Jones went away immediately, and Lloyd stopped and asked me the question still, if I had any gooseberries, after I had said I had none—I told him no, I showed him the shilling, and asked him if it was a good one—he rung it on the stone, and said it was a good one, and he wished me or him had a good many like it—he gave it me back—I never lost sight of it—he went away—I don't know whether he went in the same direction as Jones, because she crossed the road—I did not see Wilson at all—Whitlam came up and asked me what had passed—I went into a public-house with him and gave the shilling to a barman, who bit a piece out of it in my presence, and gave it to Whitlam.
Cross-examined. Q. Directly you gave change to Jones, Lloyd came up? A. Yes, I know it was the shilling Lloyd gave me, I had not another—he could not have changed it, I watched all the time he had it, and all the time the barman had it—I bit it at the time I took it, before I gave change, because I wanted to be confident it was a good one—I did not suspect it.
JOHN WHITLAM ( policeman). On the 10th July I was in company with West, and followed the prisoners—I saw Wilson go to Mrs. Leary's stall by St. George's Church—when he left it I followed him till he joined Jones and Lloyd, about two hundred or three hundred yards from the stall—at the time Wilson went to the stall, the two others were about ten yards from it—after this, I saw Jones go to Mrs. Briant's stall—while she was buying the gooseberries Lloyd stood overlooking her, with his hand in his waistcoat-pocket—as toon as he left the stall, I saw him take the shilling which had been tendered by Jones, and gingle it on the pavement, and say it was a good one—directly he left, I went up and asked the woman what she had received—we went into the public-house, and the barman bit it—I produce it—I have had it ever since—I went up to the stall directly after Jones and Lloyd left it—I lost sight of Wilson about a minute before they went away—they had been all three standing together talking—my attention was directed to Jones and Lloyd going up to the stall—I produce the second shilling.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure the barman gave you the same shilling? A. Quite—it was not out of my sight.
JOHN FIELD . The shilling produced, and the two parts of a shilling are both counterfeit, and both cast in the same mould, they are electroplated.
WILSON — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.
LLOYD — GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1723. ROBERT HEFFER was indicted for breaking and entering the warehouse of Francis Marcus Beresford, at St. John, Southwark, and stealing 25 hides, value 22 l. 10 s., his property; and JOHN LEWIS for inciting him to commit the said felony, and also feloniously receiving the said goods, knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution,
JAMES GOOD . I live in Darby's-buildings, Sloue-y-lane—I am a corn porter, but have been out of employment a week or a fortnight—Stoney-lane is in the neighbourhood of Tooley-street, which runs parallel with the Thames—I know Pickle Herring wharf, the property of Mfc Beresford—Weaver's-lane leads out of Tooley street—when you go down that in a straight fine, there is another lane to the left called Brook's-yard, that tuns off at an angle a little way further—there is a door in ftrook's-lane, leading to Mr. Beresford'f premises, and further down you come to a large gate—on Thursday evening, the 2nd of Jury, I Was in Stoney-lane, and saw the prisoner Meffer with a man named Edwards—they went from Stoney-lane—I watched them to the bottom of the lane towards the river, then lost sight of them—I saw then last in Brook's-yard, close to Mr. Beresford's premises—I did not see them do anything—on the next night, the 3rd of July, about seven o'clock, I was on the look-out in Tooley-street, and saw the prisoner Heffief in a cart with a matt named Cornally—I saw them drive down Weaver's-lane, which leads frotf Tooley-street to Brook's-yard—after the cart turned down there, I went and saw it go through the first gate leading to Brook's-yard—I there lost sight of it—it was empty when it weat down—I saw it come back in about twenty minutes laden with something covered with old mats—Cornally had left then—I followed ft—it stopped at Mr. Lewis's yard, Butlers-buildings, New-street—I understand it to be his yard—he does not live there I believe—it drew into a gateway there—I saw it leave the gateway afterwards—Heffer brought it out, he backed the horse out of the gateway—it was then empty—I followed him and the cart to the old Rose public house—I saw him
get out and go into the house—I saw by the light the name "James Barrioger Snows-fields" on the cart—I did not watch any farther that night—the next morning, Saturday, I was up about four o'clock, and about seven o'clock I went to New-street with Malt and Tyler, and saw the prisoner Lewis in the middle of the road—there was nobody with him—I had never spoke to Lewis before or seen him—I asked if he was aware of what was drawn into his yaid on the previous evening—he laughed and said, "Come and have something to drink"—I do not know if he knew Malt and Tyler—he asked us all three whether we would come and have something to drink—we went into the Queen's Head, Fashion street—Lewis called for some porter—we went into the ta room, Heffer came in some time after to the room where we were-we had three pots of porter—they were paid for by Lewis—when we were I five there, Lewis asked if Malt or any of the party were hungry—Malt said he was—Lewis put his hand into his pocket, threw half a sovereign on the table—Malt took it up and gave it to me to get change—I went to the bar and got change—Poore, the barman, gave me change in shillings—11 returned to the tap-room, and gave Malt and Tyler three shillings each, 11 kept three shillings, and the odd shilling was spent in beer—we had bad I nothing to eat, we had more to drink—Heffer and Lewis partook of the drink I—I left with Malt and Tyler—when we left, Lewis asked the whole of tu to I call on the Monday morning, at the Old Rose in Russell-street, where I had I seen the cart go to—I went there, and found Malt and Tyler there, and tk I prisoners—Lewis said the goods were removed some five miles away, nobodj I had mentioned the goods but himself—he was the first that spoke—I do not I know why he told me that—he said he had no money—I was not disappointed I at that—I came away directly—(I do not know what he gave me the thret 1 shillings for—I heard a robbery had been committed there—I did not tell I him so)—I met Lewis again in the course of the week—he said the goods were I not disposed of—he said no more about money—that was the last interview 11 had with him—I told the policeman of it in the early part of the following week.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. How long have you been a coro-porter? A. Ten years—I work at Mr. Adams'i, Mr. Goulding's, and seven! I wharfs where corn is landed—I was employed regularly about twelve months I ago by Mr. Mark Brown, a wharfinger—a fortnight ago I was employed by Newell and Keane, of Horsleydown—I was at Brown's four years as a labourer I—I left to get better employment—I got 1 l. 1 s. a week there, and nothing I else, no perquisites—Mr. Adams and Mr. Keane employed me next—we art I not employed unless we are wanted—sometimes I got more than two guise I there—some days I earned nothing—I left his employment—I gave him notice—he did not give me notice—I never bad any charge made against men I my life—I have never been in trouble, and was never charged with any crine, I further than having a few words with the police—I cannot recollect when that was—I was fined 2 l.—it was for assaulting the police—I have been employed I by the prosecutor a few days—we work from daylight to dark—I was not working on this evening in the neighbourhood of Weaver's-lane—there is one Imp I there—the lane is sixty or seventy yards long—Brook's-yard may be thence length—I went round that way on this evening, and met the prisoners—the were no persons with them—I met Cornally and Collins before them—they were walking together—this was on the Thursday—they were coming from Brook's-yard, and so were the others—I met them in Brook's-yard, which joins Weaver's-lane, about seventy yards from Tooley-street—I went there to watch them—I was not employed to do so—I said nothing to any of t I four—I walked to the water side, stood there some minutes, and returned again to Tooley-street—none of them spoke to me—I knew them—they
passed close to me—they stood there a very few minutes—on the following night I was at the top of Vine-yard, which leads to Pickle Herring-wharf—that does not run into Weaver's-lane—Brook's-yard is a thoroughfare—I did not ask for the 3 s. at the Queen's Head—he gave it me and I took it—there was no more offered—I will swear Heifer was there—I am positive he and Lewis were there on Monday—I went there, because Lewis said there should be money given—I do not know what for—I went to get it—I told what I knew about this in the early part of the week—I had part of the 3 s. left on the Monday—the last job I had I was employed by Mr. Keane, of Shad Thames—it was packing oats into hogsheads at the Tower—I got 4 s. for it—it was ten or twelve days ago—I cannot tell you when I had been employed before that—I have worked as a labourer—I gave an account of what took place to a policeman named Tate on the night of the robbery—I did not state that I was in a privy and saw the whole transaction—I passed a privy.
Cross-examined by MR. NAY LOR. Q. You asked Lewis if he was aware of what was drawn into his yard, did not he say hides? A. No—there was nobody close to me, only the other two persons—they are at large I believe—they are not here—I did not ask Lewis for anything to drink—he said, "Come and have something to drink"—on my oath I did not tell him I was one of the parties the hides belonged to, and he was to pay me—he said he had no money, not that he had no money for them, and could not pay me yet—he hat large premises there—I do not know whether he is a commission-agent—I do not know that he acted under the impression that I was one of the parties concerned in selling the hides—I did not ask him to give me something on account—I saw him an hour before he was apprehended, by order of the police—I do not know Mr. Beok—I had not asked to see a gentleman shortly before the time I went to Lewis, respecting this affair—I suspected a robbery when I saw the cart—I did not say anything to anybody—I never told Mr. Beck that Lewis had sold the hides—I did not say, Mr. Lewis was' a b----rogue, and he meant to stick to the money to my knowledge—I might say so—it was optional with him whether I had any of it—I have no recollection of saying he was a b----rogue—I do not know Beck—I did not use' those expressions to any party whatever to my knowledge—Edwards and Collins were taken up and charged with the robbery.
MR. DOANI Q. How long is it since you were fined 2 l.? A. Five or six years ago—I have never in my life been charged with felony, or with any offence.
JOSEPH PEARSON . I am foreman to Mr. Francis Marcus Beresford, wharfinger, at Pickle Herring-wharf. He has warehouses near Brook's-yard—Weaver's-lane leads from Brook's-lane into Weaver's-yard—Brook's-yard has several warehouses leading down to the Thames—it is a very long lane; and Weaver's-lane is a small lane leading into it—our inner warehouse opens by a small door near the top at Brook's-yard—passing that door you go a little distance, and then there is a gateway leading into our yard, and that yard leads to the outer warehouse—anybody can easily get over that gate—it is about nine feet high—it would facilitate anybody getting over if they were in a cart—there is a bar across the gateway, which a man can rest his legs upon—there is an entrance to the outer warehouse, which is secured by a staple and padlock—if that staple is drawn, anybody can get into the ware-house, and then into the inner warehouse—there were twenty-five hides safe there on this night—on Saturday morning, between seven and eight o'clock; I found the warehouse door broken open, the inner door unfastened, and twenty-five hides missing—I have seen the twenty-five hides in the possession of Tate—they belong to my master—I have not a doubt of them—they correspond
with them, and have two marks upon them which I can swear to—afterwards looked at the bar of the gate, and noticed a graze, as if a man had let himself down by it.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Does the gate connect the walls, or is it belonging to the buildings? A. It is between two warehouses—it connects the sides of the warehouses—there is no wall, but a gate—there is no brickwork over it—it is eight or nine feet high—we might have had 6000 or 8000 hides at that time, in different warehouses—there were 250 in the pan of the warehouse where the twenty-five were—Brook's-yard leads down to the river—it is not my duty to see the place locked up—I sent a man to lock them up—the warehouse is in the parish of St. John.
JAMES LOCKHART . I am in the service of Mr. Beresford. On Friday evening, the 3rd of July, I locked up the warehouse—it has a padlock and staple—there are gates to the yard—I fastened them with a bar acrost—I went into the inner warehouse and fastened the door leading to Brooks-yard with a bolt—I was not the first there in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. It is your duty to see it safe? A. Yes.
WILFRED DOBSON . I am in the service of Mr. Bereiford. It is my doty to open the premises in the morning—on Saturday morning, the 4th of July, I found the outer gates safe—the padlock and staple of the warehouse door were lying upon the ground—I did not go into the. Inner warehouse—there is no bolt to it.
JOSEPH PBARSON re-examined. Dobson told me what had happened. I went to the inner warehouse and found the bolt unfastened—the aides could be taken out there more conveniently than over the gate.
JAMES BARRINQER . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Snow's-fields. On Friday, the 3rd of July, when, I came home, I found my cart gone—Heifer brought it home about eight o'clock the same evening, and paid me It. on the 2nd of Aug. I had an interview with Lewis, after they had been examined at Union-hall—he came to pay me some money for hiring my I cart during the week—I said to him, "I have a summons here to attend the Old Bailey on your account; who was it fetched this cart that day, you of Heffer?"—he said, "Why I fetched it—I said, "Heffer brought it home, who fetched it, did you or Heffer?"—he said, "Why I fetched it, but I sent Heffer home with it," and he sent the money for it—he was consttaty in the habit of doing so—I have seen them together scores of times—they hire of me two or three days a week, up to the present time, and pay me to an hour, whether they have it one hour or twelve hours—I consider Le the master and Heffer the man—when Lewis came he always paid.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. How long have you known Heffin A, Two years—he is a very respectable man—they have been in the babit of paying me about 2 l. a week for the last two years—I always considered I Lewis a master—I have seen him show bills to the amount of 700 l. or 800 l.—I believe he sells leather for curriers on commission—he looks about fa customers, and then goes to different warehouses to see what they have got I have drove my cart with him to curriers' warehouses to fetch the leather he came for my cart on an average three times a week.
JAMES POORE, JUN . I am barman at the Queen's Head, Fashion-street, I and know both the prisoners. On a Saturday, early in July, between when I and eight o'clock in the morning, they came to the Queen's Head—Good was there, and Lewis—I saw some other men, but did not know them—I wrw I Lewis with beer in the tap-room—I do not know whether Good was in the tap-room—I cannot say whether they were together—Lewis was not by himself—there roust have been other persons there—I cannot say that I saw
Heffer—I did not swear that I did just now—I recollect it was Good and Lewis—I saw more men there in the tap-room—the bar looks into the tap room—there is a crook in one corner—I went into the tap-room with the beer—I cannot tell How many men were there—I did not count them—I have got other business to do—I cannot swear whether Heffer was one of them—I am sure I did not see him—he might be there and not notice him—Good brought half a sovereign to be changed—I believe he went into tho tap-room after changing it—I cannot tell whether they had more beer after wards.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Lewis often takes people there, and pays them, and gives them beer? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Did you ever see him there at seven o'clock in the morning before or since? A. I do not know.
JURY. Q. How many men were there? 4.1 cannot tell—I cannot tell How many pots of beer they had—I dare say I took in two.
WILLIAM LEWIS . I am the prisoner's brother, and live at Mr. Wallen's, a tanner's, Russell-street, Bermondsey. I have seen Heffer with my brother—on the 6th of July my brother came home in the morning, and told me be bad some hides for sale—I inquired what sort they were—he said something similar to what I had there—nothing was said about the price at thai time—I said I was not in want of that particular description of hides—on the following day I met him accidentally in the street, and after some conversation he talked about the hides, and I consented to have them sent to me—I first asked him if they were bis own—he said they were not; he had to sell them for a person or persons, I do not know which—Heffer brought them on Monday, in the middle of the day—I have given twenty of them to the police—this description of hides was constantly in the market.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Heffer acted as servant to your brother, I believe? A. I know nothing of my brother's affairs—my brother asked me to buy these in the ordinary way of business.
Cross-examined by MR. NATLOR. Q. He pressed you to buy them no more than any other person would A. No—my brother is a commission-agent—he said he had them to sell for a person or persons.
THOMAS TATE ( police-constable M 267.) On the 18th of July, in con-sequence of information, I apprehended the prisoner Lewis, at the Old Rose, Russell-street—(Heffer was in custody at the time)—I told him I took him for receiving twenty-five bides, stolen from Pickle Herring-wharf the pro perty of Mr. Beresford—he said, "The hides are all right;" that he had them from two young men, one dressed in a white smock-frock, and the other had a cap close to his head—in consequence of that, two people, Collins and Edwards, who answered the description, were taken into custody and discharged, there being no evidence against them Lewia said they told him that they picked them up one by one at the water-side, and had had them in their house some time—he afterwards told me he had sent them to Wallen's, the tanner; that is where his brother was employed—I took him to the station, went to Wallen's, and saw Lewis's brother there, who showed me the twenty five hides, and gave them up to me—I have known the witness Good some time—I never heard of his being a bad character.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR, Q. How long have you known him? Four or five years—I have seen him occasionally at work at the water side—he showed me the prisoners—he has not given me an account of the circumstances—he said something at the station-house about being in a pnvy, and witnessing the whole circumstance, and theu he contradicted it—there is a privy clnse to the gate.
Cross-examined by MR. NAY LOR. Q. Lewis told you he had them to sell
on commission for two young men, did not he? A. Yes, he told me readily every thing I inquired about them.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) I was on duty at the station-house when the prisoners were brought there—Heffer was charged with stealing, and Lewis with receiving—on hearing the charge Lewis said, in the presence of Heffer, that the hides were all right; he received them from Heffer to sell on commission; and if he had sold them, he should not have made more than a sovereign by them; and if he had known they had been stolen it would not have been worth his while—Heffer made no answer.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. How many persons were present? A. A great many—there was great confusion at the station—I will say positively that what I have said took place.
WILLIAM LEWIS re-examined. I got the hides on the Tuesday—he first offered them to me on the Monday after the Friday.
(John Molesworth Thomas, cabinet-maker; James Jenkinson, of York-place, Kent-road, leather-dresser; Joseph Fisher, glove-manufacturer, King-street, Bermondsey; William Jordan, leather-dresser, of West-street; and John Slow, constable, of Bermondsey, gave Heffer a good character.)
(James Murrel, glover, of Wild's-rents, Long-lane; Thomas James Scates, tailor, of the Borough; Henry Rodgers, lighterman, of Russell-street, Bermondsey; William Watson, carman, of Long-lane; James Cole, pump and engine-maker; and William Ax ton, of Russell-street, Bermondsey, gate Lewis a good character.)
HEFFER
LEWIS
GUILTY .— Recommended to mercy by the Jury, on account of their previous characters.
Transported for Ten Years.

[trialAccount t18460817-1725]
1725. ANN HARVEY was indicted for stealing 2 crowns, and 2 half-crowns, the monies of William Coleman, from his person.
WILLIAM COLEMAN . I am a bricklayer, and live at Church-street, Kennington. I was coming home from Newington between two and three o'clock in the morning—the prisoner came up to me, put her arms round me, and asked me whether I wanted * *—I said, "No"—she hugged round me, and would not let me go—when she got me four or five yards past the churchyard gate, she put her band into my right-hand waistcoat pocket, and took out two crowns and two half-crowns—I heard it chink as she passed it to another female—she went across to the other and said, "What do you do here while I am engaged?"—I swear I had seen the money safe not a minute before she came up to me—I had had half a pint of beer and two glasses of gin and water in the course of the evening.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were you sober? A. perfectly—I was doing nothing with the prisoner—I was trying to getawaf—I did not wish to stay with her—I felt my pocket lighter—she went across the road—I did not shake bands with her when she came back—I bad not been on the ground at all—my clothes were not all over mud at the station—I had not been with any other woman.
ROBERT PARKER ( policeman.) I took the prisoner into custody—Coleman charged her with stealing 15 s.—I saw no other girl there.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you notice any mud on Coleman's clothes? No—my attention was not called to it then, not till the depositions were
taken—he had been drinking—he knew what he was doing—he gave a straight forward charge.
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18460817-1726]
1726. JAMES WRIGHT was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged request for the delivery of goods.
RICHARD SMITH . I am a tailor, and live in Church-street, Brighton. On the 20th of June last, about half-past six o'clock in the evening, I packed up a dress coat, and directed it to "Mr. Butler, 140, Cheapside, London," and sent it by the Brighton railway—this is it—it was made in my establishment—it is a new one.
EDWARD ELSTON . I live in Cross-street, Bermondsey, and am clerk to Cbaplin and Home. The prisoner came to me, at the Brighton terminus, and said he wanted a parcel addressed to Mr. Butler, 140, Cheapside, and brought this order before the train came in—(read)—"Please deliver the bearer a parcel to come by the Brighton railway for Mr. Butler, 140, Cheapside—20th June, 1846—Brighton Railway station, London-bridge"—I gave him the parcel—he put bis initials in the book.
THOMAS PURDY . I am assistant to William Clayson, of No, 140, Cheapside. Mr. Butler is one of our travellers—on the 20th of June he was at Witham, in Essex—I know that by a letter—the prisoner did not bring any coat or parcel to our house—this order is not Butler's handwriting—there was no parcel delivered.
ROBERT BRIOGS . I am shopman to Mr. Wood, a pawnbroker. On the 20th of June, about eleven o'clock at night, this coat which I have produced was pledged at our place by a woman.
JOSHUA REEVE LAW . I am a clerk at the London-bridge Railway station, and live in the Broadway. I was present on the evening in question, and saw the parcel delivered to the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. The witnesses are wrong as to my identity; they say I applied for it twenty minutes before the train came in; How is it possible that I could apply for it before it arrived, when I am a stranger to all the parties?
RICHARD SMITH re-examined. The parcel was directed about half-past five o'clock in the afternoon of the 20th—it arrived in London at ten minutes past eight, it came by the six o'clock train from Brighton—there was no other train before this arrived, after six o'clock.
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
(The prisoner had been before convicted of felony, and there was another indictment against him.)

1727. MARGARET EVANS was indicted for stealing 2 half-crowns, 3 shillings, and 2 sixpences, the monies of James Bowler, her master.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
HELEN LEIGHTON BOWLER . I am the wife of James Bowler, a hat-manufacturer, and live at Brixton. The prisoner was in my service as cook. Oir the 28th of Jan. I gave her 9 s. 4 d. to pay to Mr. Bunce, the milkman—I gave her 3 l. at the time—I gave her gold, silver, and copper—I cannot distinctly recollect giving her any half-crowns—I might have given her coppers—(looking at her book)—I gave her 6 s. 4 1/2 d. for the butcher as well, and 6 d. for some scent, and 1 s. for some biscuits, and 6 s. 4 d. for the butter-man—I gave her all the money together—I think there were three sovereigns—the only silver I gave her was one shilling for the biscuits, and a sixpence for the scent—I cannot tell what I gave her except the three sovereigns.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460817-1728]
1728. MARGARET EVANS was again indicted for stealing 1 1 half-crown, 1 sixpence, 3 pence, and 2 halfpence, the monies of James Bowler, her master.
HELEN LEIGHTON BOWLER . I recollect the 11th of Feb.—I have it down in my book—( looking at it)—on that day I gave the prisoner 8 s. 4 d. to pay Mr. Bunce—I cannot recollect the exact coins—I cannot swear that I gave her a 5 s. piece—as far as my recollection goes, I gave her half-a-sovereign—she had to get something besides—she was to pay Mr. Bunce out of the halfsovereign.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460817-1729]
1729. MARGARET EVANS was again indicted for stealing 8 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence, the monies of James Bowler, her master.
HELEN LEIGHTON BOWLER . I have here a memorandum of the 5th of Feb., of the sum of 9 s., for the same account, to the same person—on that occasion I gave the prisoner two half-crowns and some shillings—she was to pay Mr. Bunce with the 9 s. I gave her.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Tell me what sum you gate her? A. Two half crowns, and four shillings, at least two shillings, and some sixpences—I took particular notice of the money I gave her that day (because I doubted if the bill was a true one on account of the sum—I expected the milk bill would have been more—she went out to pay it, came back, and brought me the receipt—I can swear I did not give her money to pay that and other things together—I did not give her a sovereign—I know it wa a half-crown, because I took it out of my purse, and gave it her.
WILLIAM BUNCE . I am a cow-keeper, at Brixton, and supply Mrs. Bowler with milk. On the 5th of Feb. I had an account with her—it was 9 s. up to the 1st of Feb.—I never received that money from the prisoner, and new gave her a receipt for it.
Cross-examined. Q. When did she last pay you any money? A. She has never paid me any, from the last week but one in Dec.
WILLIAM JESSE ( police-constable P 138.) On Wednesday, the 19th Of Aug., I took the prisoner—I told her what it was for, and that what she aid might be used in evidence against her—she made a reply to a woman ifce termed "Mother," that was the money she had lost—she made a statement before the Magistrate—this is the Magistrate's handwriting—( looking at the depositions—read)—"The prisoner says, 'I told Mr. Bunce I had loit the money, and would pay him as soon as I could."
WILLIAM BUNCE re-examined. She told me a day or two before sh lcfr that she had been in an omnibus, and her pocket-strings were cut, bat the did not tell me she was going to leave.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she say she would pay you? A. She said she was going to have her wages, and would pay me out of her own pocket.
GUILTY .— Confined Two Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18460817-1732]
1732. JOHN WHITE was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Edward Langford, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD LANGFORD . I am an officer in the Navy. On Monday evening, the 3rd of Aug., about nine o'clock, I was walking in Waterloo-road, and near the Victoria Theatre the officer spoke to me, and, in consequence of what he said, I felt in my pocket, and missed my handkerchief, which I had had safe about a quarter of an hour before—I have not seen it since.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. It was dark, was it not? A. Getting dark—it was about ten minutes before nine o'clock.
WILLIAM COOMBS ( police-constable L 94.) I was on duty in Waterloo-road, in plain clothes—I saw the prisoner, about twenty minutes before nine o'clock, attempt to pick a gentleman's pocket—I watched, and saw him go afterwards to the prosecutor, and draw a white handkerchief out of his pocket, just uoder the gas-lamp of the theatre—he turned, and passed it to three females—they went back, and he followed the prosecutor—I went and spoke to the prosecutor—I then turned back and took the prisoner—he said, "I bave not robbed the gentleman; I have not touched the pocket; I have been with three females"—I said, "I know you have, and you have given the handkerchief to them."
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you followed him? A. About twenty minutes—I was on the opposite side of the way—the theatre was not open at that time—there were no other persons there—I saw the prisoner speak to the females—the prosecutor was not above eight yards in front of the prisoner when I told him—the females had turned back, and gone the other way.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-sergeant L 8.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, at Newington—(read—Convicted 17th March, 1845, and confined twelve months)—I was present—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years ,
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18460817-1734]
1734. LOUISA HALL was indicted for stealing 3 yards of linen cloth value 4 s.; 16 yards of gimp, 6 s.; 3 yards of ribbon, 1 s.; 7 yards of lace 15 s.; 9 artificial-flowers, 2 s.; 24 yards of calico, 9 s.; 4 yards of satinette, 18 s.; 1 pair of gloves, 1 s.; 2 yards of muslin, 2 s. 9 d.; and 4 yards of net, 4 s.; the goods of Matthew Corke, her master.
MATTHEW CORKE . I am a draper, and live in High-street, Peckham. the prisoner was in my service for two years, within four days—she was a milliner-Moody told me something on the 7th of July—I went to his house and found a brown paper parcel containing three yards of linen-cloth, and the other articles stated in this indictment—these are them—I can swear to all but the flowers, the net and three remnants of ribbon—this gimp is a pattern that we had at the time, and we have missed such—I then made out an account of articles which the prisoner had had, and gave it to her—I asked her if she had had any other articles to enter, either herself, or from any other person—she told me she had not—that she had entered everything up—I asked her to read over the bill
in my presence—which she did, and she said she had no doubt that bill was correct, she had no other articles to enter—I then asked her if she had purchased any gimp—she told me she had had six yards of gimp that morning—I said, "Then show it to me"—she said it was on her polka—I asked her to show it me, and then she said it was not on her polka, she had sent it away—she had no business with these articles.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is the value of the whole of these articles? A. About 5 l.—she was at liberty to have articles if they were properly entered—she could not serve herself—she ought to have gone to a second party to have got the goods, and had them properly entered—she might go to any other party, or to myself—she had been in my service about four days short of two years—I think she became of age about a week after she was taken—I did not pay her any salary—she was an improver or apprentice, articled for two years as an improver, without salary—before I put this question to her, Moody had told me that he had seen her take a piece of silk out of the shop that morning—I only know her sister—I had a character with the prisoner—I felt it my duty to prosecute her.
Q. Have you not said you would not have prosecuted her at all if it had not been for an oath you took 1 A. No—I never took an oath on this subject—I was once at Weymouth—I did not there register an oath in Heaven that nothing should ever induce me not to prosecute to the utmost—I found sone one had been robbing me, and I challenged them all in the shop, and madei solemn oath that I would never forego to prosecute if I found any of then taking things—I did not assign that as a reason why I prosecuted her—I Bd not tell Moody so—I told him I had taken an oath many years ago—I did not tell him that was the cause of my prosecuting in this instance—I do not know How came to tell him I had taken an oath—it came out all on I sudden—I was aware that the prisoner's sister is a milliner—it did not occur to me that it might hold out a temptation to a young girl who was working at my place, and who had no wages—it is not usual to give improven or apprentices a salary—we require them to be neatly dressed, but not extravagantly—I understood from her aunt, or from herself, that she had a certain sum coming in to maintain herself—I knew her sister was a working milliner—I did not know where she lived—she has called at my house to see the prisoner, and she might have been a customer at one time—this is the polka the prisoner was in the habit of wearing when going out—it has nothing belonging to me on it—this is a piece of calico—this is the portion of silk thatwai cut off from the piece—this is seven yards and a half of lace—there are thing entered to the prisoner in my books—I have a person in my employ named Baker—I did not know that the prisoner was in the habit of having thing, and not entering them at the time, but entering them afterwards—this the gimp—here are sixteen yards and three-eighths of it, and there are only fly yards entered—Moody went to the Parcel Delivery Company, got the parcel and took it to bis house, and I went and looked at it—I think these thing are worth 65 s. or 66 s.—I heard that the prisoner when she was of age wow come into the possession of property—I do not know what.
REBECCA CHEESEMAN . I am in the service of Mr. Corke. I took spared on the 7th of July to the Parcel Delivery Company—the prisoner gave it—I do not know what it contained, nor whether it was directed.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did she prive it you? A. Just alter breakfast—it was not in the shop—it was in the millinery-room—theretn nobody there but her and me—Miss Baker was in the shop, which is next the millinery-room—I did not go through the shop, but the back way—I be ten years old next month.
GEORGE WILLIAM MOODY . I am assistant to Mr. Corke. On the 7th of July I saw a parcel sent away—I thought there was something wrong, and I directly called Mr. Corke's attention to it—I asked if he thought he had got all honest people about him—I saw the prisoner give the parcel to Cheeseman—I bad seen her come into the shop, and take this parcel of satinett from the shop into the work-room—I went to the Parcel Delivery Company, and found a parcel—I opened it, and called Mr. Cork's attention to it.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you remember having a conversation with Mr. Corke about an oath that he took at Weymoutb? A. I have heard him speak of an oath he bad taken at Weymouth, that he would prosecute to the utmost everybody that was robbing him—he said something of the sort, that that was his reason why he prosecuted the prisoner—I never had any quarrel with the prisoner—she never, to my knowledge, charged me with beating the apprentices when I was drunk—she might have charged me with being drunk—I have beaten Sharpe—she might have spoken about that—I cannot remember it.
GEORGE JOHNSON ( police-constable P 142.) Mr. Corke called me in on the 7th of July, to take charge of the prisoner.
MR. CLAEKSON called
MISS BAKER. I am in Mr. Corke's employ—I have had articles from the shop against Mr. Corke's rule—I have omitted to enter them for a day—never more than that—I receive a salary—I have not perquisites on everything that n sold—I have on remnants of ribbon—I have never omitted to enter anything in the book longer than a day, to my knowledge—I have seen the prisoner take things, and had them entered one day afterwards—we who are in the shop have the privilege of taking-anything we want, if it is entered—I tare taken them—I have seen the prisoner take things, and she has told me to enter them—I saw her do that on the morning she was taken into custody—I have seen her sister—I heard she was a milliner—I have heard the prisoner was coming into possession of 100 l. when she became of age.
NOT GUILTY ,
[trialAccount t18460817-1735]
1735. LOUISA HALL was again indicted for stealing 3 yards of crape, value 5 s.; 2 yards of silk, 9 s.; 2 pairs of boots, 8 s.; 3 pairs of shoes, 10 s.; 20 yards of quilling, 1 s.; 6 yards of lace, 1 s.; 2 pairs of gloves, 1 s.; and 30 yards of ribbon, 1 s.; the goods of Matthew Corke, her master.
GEORGE JOHNSON ( police-constable P 142.) I was called into the prosecutor's house—I went up stairs with him and the prisoner—these article were found in the prisoner's box, in her bed-room—while Mr. Corke was looking over another box, I saw the prisoner take this parcel and pnt it into her pocket—she took these boots and shoes and placed them under the foot of the bed—after Mr. Corke had done looking at other boxes, I called his attention to the prisoner's pocket, and then to the foot of the bed.
MATTHEW CORKE . The prisoner was in my service—these articles are my property—I can swear these boots and shoes, and crape, and silk, and blond quillings have not been entered.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How do you know these boots and shoes? A. They bear my private mark—they are new—there are shop-tickets on these other things—I never missed any of these things—I never put any question to the prisoner till I had been told by Moody that he bad seen the parcel go out—the prisoner was a person of great trust.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.— Judgment Respited.

ROBERT DEIGHTON . I am a pawnbroker. [trialAccount t18460817-1737]
1737. THOMAS COOMBS was indicted for stealing 8 sixpences and 3 groats; the monies of George Tranent; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
SARAH REED . I am bar-maid to Mr. George Tranent—he keeps the French Horn, at Lambeth-walk. On the morning of the 3rd of Aug. I saw the prisoner behind the counter—the till was open, and his hand was in it—he had no business there—I ran to catch him—he got off, and I called the constable Goose—he took him—I examined the till, and found it empty—there ought to have been eight sixpences and three fourpenny pieces in it.
Prisoner. She never called any policeman at all; she never saw me in tht house. Witness. I found him behind the counter and bis hand in the till—he threw the money down.
THOMAS GOOSE ( police-constable L 160.) I saw the prisoner come any from the bouse—he ran down a court about four yards long and dropped the money from bis hand down in the mud—I found eight sixpences and two 4 d. pieces—I am sure he dropped it—there was nobody else down the court—I Wl watched him before he went into the house.
SARAH WEDDELL . I manage the business of Mr. Tranent's house. I had left eight sixpences and three 4 d. pieces in the till the night before.
Prisoner's Defence. It is false; I never was near the bouse.
RICHARD ARTHUR ( police-constable L 26.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Newington—( read—Convicted 13 th of April 1845; confined three months and whipped)—the prisoner is the person.
Prisoner. I am not the person. Witness. I have not a doubt of it—I the officer who took him—I had known him before—I am sure he is the person.
GUILTY † Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18460817-1738]
1738. JAMES ROBERTSON was indicted for stealing 68lbs. of flour value 12 s.; the goods of Thomas Cooper, his master.
THOMAS COOPER . I am a baker, and live in Bermondsey-street prisoner was in my service—I received information, and on the 31st of when he came out of the bakehouse into the shop, I told him I wanted him to fetch me 2 d. worth of milk—he seemed very much alarmed and said, "Now
Sir, before I serve the coffee-shop"—I said, "Yes now"—he turned round and ran down into the bakehouse with his basket—I followed him and detected him taking one bag of flour out of his basket, and the other I stopped him with before he took it out—I asked him what he had got there—he said a little flour, and he had taken it out of the trough.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Was not he in the habit of taking goods out, and taking a book and accounting to you weekly? A. No, I positively swear he did not—the bread was counted out from the shop every day, and it was his duty when he returned from the customers to account for it—it would have been fraud for him to take out a pound of flour, unless I or some person gave it him—if he had an order for flour it would have been bit duty to ask for it—he had conducted himself well—he had been with me about eight months—I have no business to know the bag this flour was in—it was brought on my premises by stealth—I know the flour to be mine—it was impossible for other flour to come on my premises—this flour was kept where he could not have got it out, but by stealth—it was kept in my bakehouse—I found 27lbs. of flour in one bag and 68lbs. in the two bags—I could not have missed it.
COURT. Q. Did it correspond with your flour? A. It resembled mine—it was impossible it could be any other than mine—I am nearly blind.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year.
(The prosecutor stated the prisoner had plundered him to the value of 200 l.
[trialAccount t18460817-1739]
1739. THOMAS MACKIE was indicted for uttering a forged request for the payment of money, with intent to defraud Henry Perrin.
HENRY PERRIN . In December, 1845, I was managing clerk at Somerset-wharf, No. 58 and 59, Bank-side—it belongs to a coal company—Mr. Gibson is the head of the firm—on the 13th of Dec. the prisoner came to me—he called himself Thomas Roberts, a cooper, of Locks-fields—he asked the price of coals to the trade, saying he had taken a coal-shed there—I told him 1 l. a Ion ready money—he ordered some, and pulled out a large sheet of foolscap paper, which was a bill to James Drunkeld, Esq., for cooper's work, saying he was going to get the money—he came back a little before four o'clock and brought this check for 17 l. 10 s. signed James Drunkeld, on Messrs. Drummonds—I asked him who he was—he said a gentleman he had worked for for years, and he had often taken his checks—I asked him why he did not take it to the bankers—he said there was not time—he gave me the check—I had not money enough—I got it cashed and gave him the full change—he said he had had communication with this Mr. Drunkeld for years, and that he gave it him—I have made inquiry, and cannot find any such person—we did not take the prisoner before because we could not find him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Did you try to find him? A. I watched for him several nights at where the coals had been delivered, which he said was his residence—he gave the name of Thomas Roberts—there was no one on our wharf who knew him by that name—I sent in one load of coals, and sent in the second load the beginning of the next week, after I had passed this check—I did not know it was bad for a week after—he said Mr. Drunkeld gave him the check instead of money—I do not recollect his saying that he would get it cashed, and let me have the money on the Monday morning—I would not send the coals away till he gave me the money—he ordered two four tons—he paid me 5 l., and was to pay 3 l. more the beginning of the week, when the next load went in—he offered me the check—I got the full change for it, 17 l. 10 s.—I did not want the check—I wished him to get it cashed—he said he saw James Drunkeld write the check, and he had
taken checks of him several times—I stated that at the police-office—is my signature to this deposition—perhaps that is not stated here, but it we said so.
COURT. Q. How soon after this did you go to see if you could find him? A. I did not know the check was bad for eight or nine days; I then went, and could find no person of the name of Thomas Roberts—after I gave him the change for the check, he asked me to go to the White Bear and take 1 glass of something, as he was intimate with the landlord, Mr. Gabb—I did so, and when I found the check was bad I went to Mr. Gabb audio. quired for Thomas Roberts—he said, "That was Tom Mackie that was with I you".
ANDREW RUSH . I have been clerk at Messrs. Drummonds', the banken' for thirty years—we have no customer of the name of Drunkeld.
GUILTY . † Aged 32.— Transported for seven years.

1743. MARY DAVIS was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 8 s., the goods of Mary Ann Ferris.
SUSANNA WOOLLEY . I am a widow, and live in Portland-place, Walworth-common; I keep a wardrobe. On the 5th of August, about four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came and asked me to show her some dresses—I showed her three—she said she would purchase one, and would call in hour's time for it—I put it on the bed, for her to call again in an hour's time and pay for it—after she had selected that one I was putting the others away in a box—I had to turn round to do that—she was close to me—I saw her put something under her shawl—she left my shop before I could rise from my knees—just before that there was a shawl lying on a box, wrapped of with a bit of dimity—she had been standing close to the box—when she was gone I missed the shawl—I know it was on the box half an hour before—other person had come in between the time of my seeing it safe and missing it—she did not come back till the Tuesday following, which was a week but one day—she then asked me to let her see some dresses—I said, only those dresses I showed you when you were here last, only this half-mourning one"—I put her in mind that she had been there the last Wednesday—she said she had not been in the house for three weeks, and then said it was a fortnight—I said it was impossible to be a fortnight, for If not the dresses in my possession till the 4th of Aug.—I told her I had missed shawl, and that she took it—she said I was a lying good-for-nothing creator that I had sold the shawl, and charged her with it—I sent for the policeman—I showed her the dress that she had agreed to buy, and never came back
for—she said she knew she had bargained for that, but it was hot worth the money—I am confident she is the person.
CHARLES WEIGHT . I am nine years old; I live with Mrs. Woolley; she is my grandmother. I was at the street door when the prisoner came on the Wednesday—I saw her come oat, and she ran up the street—my grandmother missed the shawl about ten minutes after the prisoner went—I had seen my grandmother putting some fringe to the shawl a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes before the prisoner came—I was called home on the Tuesday following—I found Miss Ferris and my grandmother and the prisoner I am quite sure the prisoner is the person I had seen on the Wednesday.
JOHN MALET ( police-constable P 235.) I took the prisoner—she denied about the shawl, and said she had never had it, and had not been there for a fortnight.
Prisoner. I said I was quite innocent; I waited in the room two or three minutes.
SUSANNAH WOOLLEY re-examined. The shawl belonged to Mary Ann Ferris, who keeps a grocer's shop three doors below me—the gave me the shawl to match the fringe—I had the care of it
NOT GUILTY .

1744. HENRY WELCH was indicted for stealing 1 waistcoat, value 2 s., the goods of William Henry Hancock:—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Charles Hancock.

Mr. Ward's, at Clapham—I was playing at cricket on Clapham-coramon on Thursday evening, the 30th of July—I put my waistcoat amongst some other clothes on the grass, and while I was playing it was gone—this is it—my father's name is Charles Hancock—he pays for my clothes—I did not see the prisoner there.

[trialAccount t18460817-1745]
1745. MICHAEL HYDE was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, 2 half-sovereigns, 7 shillings, and 1 5 l. Bank note; the monies of Charles Beer Simmonds, from his person.
CHARLES BEER SIMMONDS . I am a tanner—and I live in Long-lane, Bermondsey—I have known the prisoner four or five years—on Friday morning, the 24th of July, I met him in Long-lane, between twelve and one o'clock—I was a little the worse for liquor, but knew perfectly what I was about—it was between twelve and one that I met the prisoner—he wished me to give him a drop of something to drink—I was perfectly agreeable, and we went together to the Kentish Drover's public-house, in the Kent-road—we went to no other house—we went in there about four o'clock in the morning—I had. been walking about with him from between twelve and one till about four o'clock—we had had nothing to drink—we tried at some public-bouses, and they were shut—he took me a considerable distance, and I would not go any further—we came back to the Kentish Drovers and ordered some coffee—I drank a little, but not a great deal of it—I drank it out of a saucer—I helped myself—the prisoner held the saucer sometimes—he wished me to drink more,' and held the saucer to my mouth—I told him I had had sufficient—if he was inclined to drink more he might have it himself—I had a 5 l. Bank of England note,
a sovereign, two half-sovereigns, and from six to ten shillings in my left hand pocket—I did not have anything else to drink—after had the coffee I fell asleep—I awoke I think about nine o'clock—the prison was then gone—my purse was handed to me by one of the pot-boys—there was no money in it—I had received the 5 l. note from a loan society—i got of Mr. Hinge.
Prisoner. We had something to drink at Star-corner; we did not go to the Kentish Drovers. Witness. We did go to the Kentish Drovers.
JONATHAN STACEY . 1750. WILLIAM HAWKINS was indicted for stealing on the 29th of July. 1 shirt, value 3 s.; 3 table-cloths, 4 s.; 2 table-covers, 2 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 4 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 apron, 6 d. the goods of John Lee: 3 pairs of stockings, 3 s.; 1 pair of drawers, 2 s.; and two handkerchiefs, 3 s.; the goods of Robert Scrivener: and 1 shift, 1 s.; and 2 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; the goods of Jane Bettesworth.
JANE BETTSWORTH . I live with Mr. John Lee, a grocer, at No. 8, Dorset-place, Old Clapham-road. On Tuesday, the 28th of July, about two o'clock, I hung out in his garden two table-cloths, three pairs of stockings, a pair of totters, four pairs of drawers, a cotton waistcoat, a sheet, not a shirt, and light pocket handkerchiefs, and there was a shift in the wash-house—I saw them all safe on Wednesday morning at two o'clock, as I was sitting up for Mr. Lee half-past six the same morning, I missed the articles—I found the wash-house door wide open—it had been shut the night before—I observed a chair by the wall, by the side of the back door, between Mr. Notley garden, which is next to ours, and the Clapham-road—a person could get from there to our garden, and get out by going back again—these the articles—some of them belong to Mr. Lee—this cotton handkerchief belongs to Mr. Robert Scrivener, who is lodging there—this shift is my own, and was in the wash-house—the other things were out to dry.

about forty yards from Mr. Lee's house—I saw the prisoner come don from the side of Mr. Notley's wall, directly from the place where some one bid got over the wall—I noticed a frail basket under his arm—I followed him—he went in the direction of the Swan, at Stockwell—he looked round—I followed him to the Swan—I have every reason to believe he is the roan—I went on Wednesday night and saw where some one had got over the wall.
WILLIAM GLEED ( police-constable V 276.) On that Wednesday morning, at a quarter before four, I was near the Swan, and saw the prisoner within basket, going in the direction of Clapham—he asked me the way to Brixtoo, and said he had been sleeping in a garden, but he was glad he had not lost the basket—he afterwards said he had got over the wall at the Greyhound and had been asleep—he did not say he had been turned out of the Grey, hound, where he had been drinking—the basket appeared t ob ef all of some thing—there was a kind of handkerchief or apron on the top of it—on the 30th of July, in consequence of what I heard, I went to Mitcham, and found the prisoner reaping in a corn field—he had his white trowsers and shirt on, and that was all—I charged him with stealing linen in the Clapham-road—k said, "Linen, what linen—I took no linen; so long as you don't take me for a deserter, I will go with you"—he went about ten yards, and pretended to be looking for his jacket—he then set off running—I ran after him about half way across the field—he then turned, and gave me a blow on the bead—I stopped him till another constable came and assisted me—I am certain that the prisoner is the same man I had seen in the morning—after I took him he said he had been to the Dorset Arms, and went to sleep there, andhad left his stock there.
RICHARD GOLDING ( police-serjeant V 31.) I am stationed at Merton. On the 30th of July, Gleed told me something, and I took him to a corn field I saw the prisoner there—Gleed tried to take him—he ran away—he was I pursued and taken—he went home and cleaned himself, and then he said bt would not go to military duties—I said it was not that he was wantedfor it was for stealing linen—he said, "Linen, I could soon show you what I I had with me"—I went to M'Leish's, and found a basket and these articles.
THOMAB EMMIRSON ( police-serjeant V 10.) I went with Golding to M'Leish's, and found these things—on the morning of the 31st of July Im the prisoner at the station—I told him we had found the linen he wai charged with, at his sister's in Acre-lane—he said he knew uothing abort I any linen, we could bring nothing to his charge
ANN M'LEISH . I am a widow—the prisoner is my brother. On Wednesday morning, the 29th of July, he came to my place between four and five o'clock in the morning, and brought a basket and these articles—he left themand said he would call again in a few days—he staid about half an hour or three I quarters, and went away—I did not know what was in the basket before K went—the policemen came, and I let them have it.
Prisoner's Defence. I came out from barracks, and was drinking at the I Swan; I then went to the Dorset Arms, and had a pint of beer; I went round the corner, took my stock off, and went to sleep; I awoke, and went into the road; I found the basket with these articles; I did not know what was in it.

1753. JOHN ROBERTS was indicted for embezzlement
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SAWYER . I am a greengrocer, and live in Union-road, Clapham. Major Sheppard resides at Elm-house, Clapham-common—he has two-of three large gardens attached to his house—the prisoner was his gardener—I ever saw him before last July—I met his wife, and in consequence of wkat she said, I went down to Major Sheppard's cottage, where the gardener resides—I prisoner—he told me he had got fruit to sell in the garden, black currants, red currants, aud white currants, gooseberries, and
other things—I said I would bave it, and I gave him 2 s. deposit—he said I should have all the fruit in the garden— from the 10th to the 21st of July I purchased to the amount of 3 l. 17 s. 6 d.—I paid him for every thing as I fetched it away—I am quite sure I had to that amount—the whole amount I had was 4 l. altogether, up to the 25th of July.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you lived where you do? A. Two months, under Mr. Gardner—I worked before for Mr. Finch, a carpenter, at Wimbledon—I bave been a bricklayer's laboured a carpenter's labourer—I have no account of the sale of this fruit—I took it as I take it in Covent-garden, and paid for it—what I had between the 10th and the 21st of July was 3 l. 17 s. 6 d.—I think I am wrong in that ststemet, it was 2 l. 17 s. 6 d. Is went to the prisoner, after the major had come bow, but I did not know it—I then wanted some fruit, which the prisoner refuted to let me have—I had been there a few days before, and he said there were ax half sieves of red and black currants; and when I went, he said I wts to have the last, and that was all, and be would not let me go into the ganfca to see—he pushed me out, as if I was not to dare to go in—he once wanted me to buy some hay—I asked him to let me have a bit for a sample, that I might show it to somebody that knew it better than myself—he said be bad shsj* stack to sell, and be bad a borse to sell, and Major Sbeppard wanted lit if it—I did not give him a shilling for the little bit of bay—he refused to itf me have it—I have a stable—I did not ask him to let roe bave a few ateos to pave it—I did not say that I would be d—d if I would not do for him—I told him the last time, when he sold me only one half-sieve, that fld pull him to Wands worth, because the agreement was, I should have all fruit in the garden; and the last week be would not let me go I garden, to see what there was.
MR. PARRY. Q. You did not keep an account of the exact smut you paid him day by day? A. No—I hare no doubt whaterer that I felt prisoner 4 l. altogether for Major Sheppard.
FRANCIS ADAMS . I am a confectioner and pastry-cook, and live on Clapham-common. I know the prisoner—I purchased about three halfsieves of gooseberries and currants of him—the amount of them was 7 s. 6 d.—paid him for them—I took no receipt.
Cross-examined. Q. When was it? A. On the 11th of July I purchased 5 s. worth; the other half-crown I cannot say when it was—it was at my wn house—I fetched the goods as I wanted them, in the morning.
EDMUND SHEPPARD . I am a major in the Royal Artillery, on half-pay—I reside at Elm-cottage, Clapham—the prisoner entered my service on tto 13th of May, and left ou the 1st of Aug.—on the 18th of June I went ottrf town, with my family—I sent instructions to the gardener in writing.
JAMES BUCHANNAN . I am in the service of Major Sheppard—I was at Clapham in June, when the family left—the prisoner was the gardener—I went with a letter to him on the 30th of June, when I received it—on the night the prisoner was taken he told the constable that he had paid the major 2 l. 8 s. 3 d., and that was all he had received.
The letter contained the following sentence:—"Tell the gardener, and show him this letter, he had better dispose of the strawberries and currants, and vegetables; be can account to his master for them."
EDMUND SHEPPARD re-examined. I returned to town on the 20th of July—I sent the prisoner a verbal order on the 21st to discontinue selling anything—I did not see him for some days afterwards—about the 27th of July he paid me 2 l. 8 s. 3 d., and merely said that was the proceeds of the sale of the vegetables and fruit—I did not ask him any further questions.
Cross-examined. Q. How did this man come into your service? A. I wrote to Sir "William Jolliff for bis character—he bore a good character—honost, sober, and trustworthy—he lived with me exactly nine weeks—I have had nine gardeners within the last four years—the prisoner left me on the 1st of Aug.—there was no charge against him then.
Q. Do you remember sending him away in consequence of a quarrel with the foot-boy, who was constantly bothering him to have the key? A. He was not sent away absolutely in consequence of that down several times for things, and he refused to open the gate I was forced to go down, and the prisoner said he did not choose to be called "gardener," and he did not choose to have so much plague and trouble, and J turtied him away—it was on the 1st of Aug.—I turned him out in the hall-strom—I bad the policeman there merely for the purpose of keeping the peace, the prisoner was making use of abusive language—when I gave him 'riotte to quit, he came up to me and said he thought he was coming in the ifemto of a gentleman, and I was a low blackguard; and his wife came out of the cottage and used the same words—when I gave him notice to quit, he round and said, "I will go on Saturday"—I said, "Of course in that case you will not have the week's wages."
MR. PARRY. Q. You gave him notice to quit on the 29th? A. Yes, "and he on the Saturday—I believe the policeman did nothing towards oat, he only told him to go.
WILLIAM MATTHEW SHEPPARD . I am the son of Major Sheppard—I paid the prisoner his wages when he left on the 1st of Aug., by my fathers direction—on the day he was taken up he told me he had received 2 l. 8 s. 3 d. for vegetable and fruit, and that was all.
NOT GUILTY .

1756. JAMES HICKMAN was indicted for bigamy.
JOSEPH ROUSE . I am parish-clerk of the church of St. Mary, Haggerston, in Burnswick-street, Hackney-road I have a certificate of a marriage which I copied from the register of marriages in that church—I examined it, it is correct—it
relates to a marriage which took place on the 15th of May, 1842—I "was present at the marriage—I recollect the female to whom this certificate refers was Mary Keefe, she is now in this Court, I know her to be the person who was married to some man, by the name of James Creek, by Mr. Neath, who was curate at that time.
CHARLOTTE AXE . I am wife of Samuel Axe—I was present at that marriage on the 15th of May, 1842—the prisoner is the man who was then married to Margaret Keefe—she is now in Court.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You knew her very well? Yes—the did not run away from the prisoner, he ran away from her.
WILLIAM ARCHER . I am parish clerk of St. Mary Rotherhithe. I produce a certificate of a marriage in that church—I saw it taken from the register on the day of the marriage, and I have seen it since—it is a true copy of the entry in the register—I was present at the marriage—I am quite certain the prisoner is the man that was married to a young woman named Caroline Gower, on the 6th of July, 1846—he was married in the name of James Hickman—( certificates read.)
FREDERICK PICKERING ( police-constable K 87.) I apprehended the prisoner—I told him what it was for—he said he thought, by being a bastard, he could marry again—he thought his marriage was not good, he could marry as many as he liked.
Cross-examined. Q. The first wife was the person who went with you? A. Yes—she gave me information—I saw Caroline Gower at the bouse—she would not come forward.
MR. HORRY called
SARAH WARD . I am the prisoner's mother. His proper name is James Hickman—he is a base-born child—his father's name is James Creek—I never lived with his father—I brought my son up—I have a copy of his christening here—it was at Sandon, in Essex—I never saw the register, but I can swear he was christened "James Hickman"—my sister sent this certificate to me.
COURT. Q. What name did the prisoner go by? A. James Creek—he did not come to me to tell me he was going to be married, or I should have told him better—he did not know to the contrary—he did not assume that name to go and get married—he did not know any difference till the dijf after he was married when I went and said he was married wrong—he did not know but it was right.
MR. HORRY. Q. Did you know that he and his first wife were parted? A. I did not know it for a long time after they were parted—I know nothing of his first wife.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
