[trialAccount t18460817-1758]
1758. THOMAS JENKINS was indicted for stealing 1 ring, value 15 s. 2 lancets and case, 5 s.; and 1 seal, 5 s.; the goods of Samuel Abarrow Way, his master.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL ABARROW WAY . I am a surgeon, and live in Park-street, South-wark.
I know the prisoner—he was with me—I examined my stock in consequence of a communication made to me by Mrs. Hill, where the prisoner lodged, at No 6, Napoleon-place, Bermondsey—I went there, and wasshown a box—I know that to be the prisoner's lodging—I found in the box a cork-screw, which had been in my possession seven or eight years—it turns up like a harp, and a lancet, it is marked with the name of Brown inside, and I had several more of the same sort, and several silver and elastic catheters—I made further inquiries, and met with Emily Jones eventually—I went to Mr. Mullins, a pawnbroker, in the Wai worth-road—I there found this ring—I know it particularly, because the rim of it comes off, and it has done so for the last twelve months—I have had it put on twice—it has been in ray possession twenty years—I have been in the habit of wearing it sometimes for six months together—I missed this ring while the prisoner was in my employment—I afterwards saw a lancet-case—this is it—I know it—I have had it ten or twelve years.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. When did you miss this cork-screw? A. Perhaps a fortnight after the prisoner left me—I did not miss the lancet-case—I bought this ring second-hand—I believe the prisoner came to me about the 15th of May, and left on the evening of the 17th of June—I am a surgeon—I am not a member of the College—I belong to the Society—I am an apothecary and surgeon—I keep a shop—I deal in drugs and medical herbs—I am not what is familiarly called a "quack doctor" to my knowledge—I do not sell patent medicines—the prisoner came to me to take a shop of me which I had to let—that was the first I knew of him—I never engaged to give him any specified sum for his services, but very likely I should have done so—he was assisting me—he had his board—he was not assisting me the whole time—a fortnight or three weeks he was ill, and one day he went away and stopped all night, and came the middle of the next day—he went on towards the close of the time, and a female called—her name I believe was Emily Jones—he went home with her, and did not return till the next evening—he had a lodging of his own at the next door—my initial* are scratched at the end of one of the lancets—the prisoner was not in the habit of using these—this case was kept locked up in my desk in the shop, and 1 had a man to put a lock on, and the man was two hours or two hours and a half, while the prisoner held up the cover—the rim of this ring comes off.
Q. I see here is the name of Nathaniel H. Towle inside it. A. you know the name better than I do—I never looked at it, or if I have looked at k tenor twelve years ago, I do not recollect it—I went to Mr. Mullens, a pawnbroker—I do not recollect that I was asked if there was any writing on the ring, and that I said no—I have had the ring twenty years—I cannot see the writing on it, it is so small—the prisoner asked whether I meant to give him anything—I said, "Before you was in my employ a week, you borrowed 10 s. of me to repay me, when you had a remittance"—I tokl him I did not consider there was anything due to him—upon that refusal an action has been brought against me, and after bringing that action I gave the prisoner into custody.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was it after the action was brought you found these things? A. Yes.
EMILY JONES . I live at No. 18, Lyon-street, Kent-road—I am unfortunate—I knew the prisoner while he was in the service of Mr. Way—I received this ring and this lancet-case from the prisoner while he was in Mr. Way's service—I gave them to my sister to pawn—I am quite sure these are the articles I received from the prisoner—I received the duplicates of them
from my sister—I gave them to a witness who is here—this seal was left promiscuously, by the prisoner, on my table.
WILLIAM MORTON ( police-sergeant P 7). I went to the prisoner's lodgian at Mrs. Hill's—I saw the prisoner there—Jones handed me this seal.
MR. WAY re-examined. These articles have been mine for some years.
WILLIAM GREGORY . I was employed by the prosecutor. At his reqtat I went to some pawnbrokers, and went to the prisoner's lodging—I saw him given in charge—in consequence of some inquiries, I was led to a tobaccomfi shop, and went to Mr. Viney'a, and from him I found Emily Jones—ihe delivered these duplicates to me, and this letter.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. Nothing at present—I hm settled one or two law proceedings for the prosecutor—I was a banfari clerk, and I have been an attorney's clerk.
ROBERT FISHER . I am assistant to Mr. Mullins, a pawnbroker, it Walworth. I received these articles in pawn, the one on the 13th, and theofer on the 17th of June—I advanced 1 s. 6 d. on one, and 95. on the other—they were pawned by a female; I do not know who—I should imagine I could not have had this ring for twenty years without seeing the writing.
GUILTY .— Confined Six Months:
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

1759. JAMES SMITH was indicted for that Henry Harland, the younger, being in the dwelling-bouse of John Harland, did steal 1 watch, value 4 l.; and 1 coat, 2 l. 10 l.; the goods of Richard Harland: 1 mantle, 1 l.; 1 shawl, 1 l.; the goods of John Harland: 1 waistcoat, 5 s.; 1 belt, 2 s.; 3 scarfs, 6 s.; 1 shawl, 2 s. 6 d.; and 3 shirt-collars, 6 d.: and having committed the said felony, did feloniously and burglariously break out of the same dwelling-house: that the prisoner did incite him the felony aforesaid to do and commit, and feloniously received the same goods, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. RYLAHD conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD HARLAND . My father's name is John Harland—I have a brother named John, and another named Henry—we all live with my father, in Union-row, Old Kent-road. My brother Henry went to bed on the night of the 5th of July—in the morning I was awoke, and he was gone—he had slept in the same bed with me—I missed a watch and a coat—I took a deal of pains to find Henry—I saw him again about ten days after, about the Wednesday week—he was then in custody—the policeman produced the articles to me which I had missed.
COURT. Q. What time did you go to bed that night? A. Between eleven and twelve o'clock—all was then shut up safe—our house is in the parish of St. Mary, Newington—the watch was worth 4 l., and the cost 1 l. 10 s.—there were other things gone belonging to my father and mother, to the amount of 7 l. or 8 l.
PRISCILLA HARLAND . I am the wife of John Harland. On the morning of the 6th of July I missed a great-coat of my husband's, a mantle and shawl of my own, and various articles belonging to my son John.
JOHN HARLAND . I am a son of the last witness. On the morning of the 6th of July I missed three coats, four or five pairs of trowsers, five waistcoats, and some other things—I saw a handkerchief produced by Kerney—I had not missed it, but I afterwards found I had missed such a thing—I went down to Brighton, and I saw my brother's watch and a coat at Mr. Lewis's, the pawnbroker—I shut up our house safely on Sunday night, the 5th of July, about half-past eleven o'clock.
HENRY HARLAND . I am fourteen years old. On the night of Sunday, the 5th of July, I went to bed with my brother in my father's house—I went away at four o'clock the next morning—I had been living with Nosworthy and Wells, in Coleman-street, for about seven months—I found the prisoner there—when I had been there about six weeks, he asked whether I had got a pair of trowsers I could let him have—I told him no—he still kept asking me and bothering me, and I took a pair of my own trowsers from home, and sold them to him—he said he would give me 1 s. for them, but he did not—he gave me 4 d.—some time after he asked me for another pair—he said these were almost worn out, he wanted a pair for Sunday—I took a pair of my brother's, and sold them to him for 4 d.—he paid me for them—these tort of things went on several times—I took various articles—I gave him some and kept some—some nights before I left home I saw the prisoner—I bad a sovereign of my master's, and he was afraid I should confess—he advised me to divide the sovereign with him—he said it would be better for me to come away from home, for if Mr. Nosworthy found out about the sovereign, I should be transported—he advised me to go away, and go to sea—he told me to get plenty of things, and plenty of money, and he would try and dispose of the things, and give me some of the money, and keep half of it—it was in consequence of that I took all these things on the Sunday night and ran away—I went to the prisoner's lodging at half-past five in the morning—I found him getting up when I got there—I had got ail these things with me—I gave him some of the oldest things—he went out to work, and alter that hit wife pawned a coat for 14 s.—the prisoner came home, and said there was a piece of work, a policeman was after me, and I had better be off—he advised me to go to Brighton—I carried some of the things away with me—I took the watch and a coat of my brother's—I went to Brighton—I pawned the watch and coat in Ship-street, Brighton.
COURT. Q. Do you mean to swear that the prisoner excited you to do all these things? A. Yes, it was in consequence of his advice I did it.
JAMES KERNEY ( police-sergeant P 36.) I heard of this robbery, and on, the 11 tb of July I went to the prisoner's lodgings—I told him to be particular in what he said, for I was sure if Harland was not there he knew where he was—He said, "I know nothing about him, I have not seen him for six week"—I found on Henry Harland this powder-flask—he said the prisoner gave it him—the prisoner said he knew nothing about the powder-flask, be never had such a thing in his possession—I told him I would take him into custody on suspicion of receiving this property—he said he knew nothing about it—he told me he gave his wife a coat to pawn, which was pawned at Mr. Telford's for 14 s.—I went there and found this coat—it is the one that John Harland has identified.
JOHN HARLAND re-examined. This is my coat, it is one of the things my brother went off with that morning.
MARY ANN ROBINSON . I lived with the prisoner as his wife—I pawned this coat—Henry Harland came and said he had got no money—that his father and mother sent him out to go to sea—he gave me the coat to pawn—I gave him the money and the duplicate—he came twice to our house—he wid he would ask his mother for some old clothes, and I believed it—I took the scarf and the cloak for myself, and said I was very much obliged to him"—I have seen this powder-flask—it belonged to my husband—he gave it to Henry Harland for the sake of his giving me the scarf and cloak.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18460817-1760]
1760. JOHN HART was indicted for unlawfully and feloniously and wounding Robert Larkin, the younger, on his right arm, left eye-lid, right hand, with intent to do him some bodily harm.—2nd Count, to resist his lawful apprehension and detainer.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT LARKIN, JUN . I live at Stamford-place, Hatfield-street, Surrey, On the 5th of Aug. I had been to the police-office with my wife on a charge against Mrs. Truebody, who is I believe the sister of the prisoner's wife—she was bound to keep the peace—I returned to Stamford-place—I saw Mn, Hart—the prisoner was not there—my father was there—he had been having some words with Mrs. Hart—there was some abuse of my father, and I went for Kidney the policeman—I returned with him—as we came out of Stamford-street, to cross to Stamford-place, Mrs. Hart crossed to go to Upper Ground-street—in two minutes she returned with the prisoner Hart and her own brother Finn—the policeman was in uniform—Finn struck me at the top of Stamford-place, as I was standing by the officer, without any words having passed between us—I did not strike him again—I was knocked senseless—I was struck on the left side—I reeled with it—then Hart struck me a blow—I saw Parsons—he was called by the officer to assist him—he came, and he was attacked by Hart—I was at that time in a state of stupefaction, bat I saw Hart strike Parsons—I saw Parsons on the ground, and Hart on the top of him, and Hart's wife likewise—Parsons was dragged away from then senseless by some people—there were twelve or fourteen people assembled—when Parsons was dragged away, Hart attacked me a second time, and we struggled together, to Hatfield-street—he threw me—I got up—He threw me again, and kicked me—I got up, and got hold of him, and beings longer-armed man than he is, I succeeded in holding him off, and fat recovered myself—by this time the crowd had increased—there were from thirty to fifty persons, I should think—I held Hart at arm's length, and Mrs. Truebody, his sister-in-law, came and tried to rescue him—by palling him we came more closely in contact—Finn, at this time, struggled with the policeman, and Finn came and kicked me—the policeman tore him from me—Hart and I struggled together for three or four minutes—Hart then tad this knife in his hand—I think it was given him—I had not seen it in his hand—I had not then said anything about giving him in charge—the policeman had told me to hold him till more assistance came—I then saw the knife in his hand, and I felt a sharp instrument go into my arm, bat I did not think he was stabbing me, till he shoved it down my left eye, down my cheek—he found I did not let him go, and he cut me across my forefinger into the bone—that cut caused me to release him—he got from me, but did not get more than ten yards—I seized him again—he was shutting the knife np at the time, and put it into his jacket-pocket—I succeeded in retaining him after all—we struggled again heavily together—I succeeded in throwing hi down—he did not get away—the policeman came, and I gave him into charge—we then proceeded about a hundred yards—his wife, Mrs. Hart then came, and attacked me a second time—she bit me through the finger the officer let Hart go, to come to my assistance, and Hart escaped—I did not see him afterwards taken.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. There had been a quarrel between the Larkins and the Harts? A. There were some words occurred, which led me to take Mrs. Truebody up—she lodged two doors from me—she
has recently lost her husband—I made my complaint before the Magistrate—he held her to bail—my father returned with me to my own place—on our return I saw Mn. Hart—she began quarrelling—her abusive language to my father drew me out—my father had a stick—he is very much afflicted with the gout—I did not see him threaten to strike Mrs. Hart with the stick—I did not hear her complain that he committed a brutal assault on her with his stick—it was a large thick stick—I swear that in my presence he did not strike her at all—she left and fetched her brother and the prisoner—when he came up he did not ask why his wife had been struck—nothing had happened to Mrs. Hart—I heard her say, as I passed my father, and said I would fetch a policeman, "you can go, if you think proper; I will fetch those that shall settle you and the policeman too"—the prisoner did not come till she fetched him, and then I was struck first, not by the prisoner, but by Finn—I iought with the prisoner—I must have stood still and been killed if I had not protected myself—I held him by his jacket collar and his neckcloth—he did not say I was strangling him—he cut the neckcloth with the knife, and threw the neckcloth on the ground—I still kept hold of his collar—I felt some sharp instrument enter my arm, about two inches below the elbow—I still held him—he was struggling with me—he held the knife by the blade, and pressed it down the socket of my eye-Be then cat me across the finger—he got away, and shut the knife and put it into his jacket pocket—I seized him—we had a grand struggle—when I threw him the third time he said, "Let me go, I have bad enough."
MR. PARRT. Q. Was this fighting after you had received one or two blows from him? A. Yes, and I had been knocked against the wall—after the policeman had ordered me to keep'him in custody, he begged me to let him get up and go, and I said I would keep him till an officer came.
ROBBRT LARKIN , sen. I am the witness's father. On the 5th of Aug., about six o'clock in the afternoon, I had been to the police-court, and Mrs. Traebody was bound to keep the peace—when I went to Stamford-place, Mn. Hart abused me—my son went for a policeman, and when he returned with one, the prisoner and Finn came up—Finn struck my son and then the prisoner struck him—I saw them struggling together afterwards, and there was fighting and quarrelling going on with the others—I was assaulted but not by the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. you had a stick? A. Yes—I shook the stick at Mrs. Hart when she had scratched my daughter-in-law and pulled her hair, and she served me so—I did not give her a gentle tap with the stick—I dare say it was two feet from her.
GEORGE KIDNEY ( police-constable L 179.) I was on duty on the 5th of Ang.—Robert Larkin fetched me—I went with him—when I got to the corner of Stamford-place, Finn and the prisoner came running round the corner—they came up to Larkin, and both of them pitched into him—I was standing there—they both struck me—Finn struck me first, and I instantly laid hold of Finn—I saw Parsons—I called him to assist in holding Finn, and Parsons was thrown down by Finn, I believe—when I took hold of Finn, I said to Larkin, "Get hold of the other,"or something to that effect-1 did not see the knife used at all—there was fighting going on—I was bit in the arm by one of the women.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the first thing you saw Mrs. Hart running away? A. I saw her running from the place where old Mr. Larkin was—she seemed to be going to fetch somebody to her assistance—she went in (he direction in which I afterwards saw Finn and the prisoner come.
WILLIAM LAY . I came up when this affray was going on—I saw the prisoner
and Larkin struggling together—I saw a knife in the prisoner's hand and saw him cut his neckhandkerchief with it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see Larkin holding the prisoner by the neckcloth 1 A. I did, and the prisoner got the knife to liberate himself from bit hold—they then closed and fought again.
WILLIAM HENRY GILBERT PARSONS . I live in High-street, Lambeth. I never saw the prisoner, to my knowledge, till the 5th of Aug.—I was present when the fighting was going on in Stamford-place—I did not see the first blow—I saw the prisoner and Finn attacking Larkin—I saw Finn strike bin—the policeman laid hold of Finn, and called me to assist him—I got Larkin away from Finn, and Finn knocked me dowu—I got up and told the policeman, (as I was being knocked and kicked about by the prisoner and his wife, and some other women,) that I could do no good unless I had his staff—he told me to take it out of his pocket—I made as good use of it as I could, to protect the policeman—there were five or six persons attacking the policeman and Larkin—more of them were women than men—I kept the prisoner and his wife and the other women off—I was kicked behind by Finn—I did Dot see the prisoner have hold of Larkin after that—I was "finished off"then.
Cross-examined. I believe you were obliged to finish Mrs. Hart? A. She struck me—I would not swear that I did not strike her—I had to rescue tie staff away from her two or three times—she did not strike me with it—she could not get it away from me.
JOSEPH PRICE ( police-constable L 124.) I was not present on the 5th of Aug.—I apprehended the prisoner about one o'clock in the morning on the 6th of Aug.—I told him what the charge was—I had taken his wife first—he did not say anything, but wanted to get his wife away from me—I produce this knife—he took it out of bis right-hand jacket pocket, and gave it me.
GUILTY on the Second Count. —( See the next case.)
[trialAccount t18460817-1761]
1761. THOMAS FINN, JOHN HART, ELIZABETH HART , and MARY TRUEBODY were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT LARKIN, JUN . On the 5th of Aug., about six o'clock in the evening, I went for the policeman, Kidney, to come to Stamford-place, owing to some disturbance in which Mrs. Hart was engaged with my father—Mrs. Hart fetched Finn and John Hart—as I passed my father I heard Mrs. Hart say, "I will go and fetch my brothers,"as I understood her, "that shall do for you and the policeman too"—Finn and John Hart then came running up to me—Finn struck me on the left eye, and then John Hart struck me—I reeled back against the wall from the heavy blows I received—the policeman was close by me—while I was with John Hart, his wife, Elizabeth Hart, came up—she tore me down the side of the face from my forehead to my chin—after I gave her husband to the policeman we proceeded about a hundred yards-Elizabeth Hart came up a second time, seized me by the hair of my W forced my finger into her mouth, and bit it—the policeman, hearing me cry, released John Hart, and he got away—I did not see Truebody do anything—I was fully engaged with Hart.
Elizabeth Hart. I asked you to let go of my husband; you and your father had got him by the neckcloth; he was choking, and quite black in tbefawi I said, if you did not let him go, I would bite you; I bit you; and you had me by the hair of my head down on the ground; a woman came and said it was a shame, and you were going to murder me; you struck me. Witness. No, you tore me down my lace.
ROBERT LARKIN, SEN . I went to the police-court, and came back to my son's-Mrs. Hart came near my son's house—she did not touch me, only used insulting words-my son went for the policemen—I had a stick in my handI did not strike her with it—I was not struck by anybody; I was merely touched slightly on the cheek by Fiuo, but nothing that I wish to name, nothing to do any injury.
GEORGE KIDNEY ( police-constable L 179.) I came up with Robert Larkin, jun.—I saw Finn and John Hart come up, and both of them pitched into Robert Larkin, jun., as I was standing by his side—Finn struck him first—I laid hold of Finn, and he caught hold of Robert Larkin, jun., and threw him down—I pulled him off him—Elizabeth Hart came up, and scratched nay face and bit my arm while I had hold of Finn.
WILLIAM HENRY GILBERT PARSONS . I was present—Finn first struck me—he knocked me down, and fell on the top of me, dragging the policeman—the policeman did not fall down—John Hart and his wife both attacked me—I saw Mary Truebody calling on the others to assist in rescuing her brother-in-law—after that I had the policeman's staff to protect myself—I was kicked down by Finn again, and then John Hart came on me, and hit wife too—John Hart put his hand on my left eye, and forced it in, and I fainted—I was acting at the request of Kidney—my eye bled.
Finn. Q. While I was in the constable's hand you struck me; did I not take the staff out of your hand, and give it to the policeman?. A. I cannot say—it was at the time I was fainting.
Elizabeth Hart. He says John Hart put his finger in his eye, and it was me.
HARRIET MARGARET LARKIN . I am the wife of Robert Larkin, jun. I went to the police-office on the 5th of Aug.—my husband's father was with me—we returned to Stamford-place—Mrs. Hart used some abusive language to my father-in-law, and she pushed violently up against me while I was at the street door—I saw Finn strike my husband—I ran towards him, and Finn gave me a violent blow in my eye—I fell down, and Elizabeth Hart attacked me, and pulled my hair; my ear-rings were broken in my ears, and my comb broken in my head—I was taken home insensible.
Elizabeth Hart. you put your foot out, and I fell over it; you called me a good-for-nothing bunter, and your father came out and struck me with a stick. Witness. I never spoke to you—I do not know anything of you.
ANN LARKIN . I am the sister of Robert Larkin, Jun. I remember the 5th of Aug.—there was a great crowd between the hours of six and seven—I saw Elizabeth Hart and Truebody there—Elizabeth Hart pushed up against my sister-in-law, and called her a black w she said that she would go and fetch two men that would do for my father, and the policeman too—True-body said, "Yes, they will do for them"—when Finn struck my brother Truebody aided them—she said, "Give it to the b----b----, don't let them go—Truebody struck me on the back with her fist, and pulled my hair—Mr. Chatteris and some other persons pulled me in doors—Truebody called me a b----w----out of Tooley-street—I was standing at Mr. Chattern's door—I had not given any provocation—I said nothing to anybody.
Elizabeth Hart. You did not say that Mrs. Truebody struck you; you told the gentlemen that I followed you, and pulled your hair and struck you. Witness. No, it is false; I did not see you.
Truebody. I was not there till the row was over; did you ever see me there? Witness. Yes, I felt you as well as saw you.
MARTHA SMITH . I live in Corn wall-road. On the evening of the 5th of Aug I was in Stamford-plate with the Larkins—I had just returned from the
police-court—I saw the prisoners there—Elizabeth Hart pulled the chains off my neck, tore the lace off my frock, and pulled my hair—this was after Elizabeth Hart went to fetch some person, and this occurred when she returned—I had not said anything to her—I was standing at the door by Mn, Larkin—she had asked me to fetch a loaf for tea.
WILLIAM CHATTERIS . I am employed in Stamford-place. On the evening of the 5th of Aug. I was there—I do not know the Larkins—I saw all thepri. Boners there—I saw the whole of the disturbance—I did not hear Mrs. Truebody say anything, but I saw her run after Mrs. Larkins and Miss Smith—I did not see her do anything to them—they came to me and complained, and I took them both in and protected them—I was charged by the policeman to aid and assist—I went up to Finn and he said, "you b----old—, if yw do anything to me, I will kick your b----guts out"—he kicked me violently on the knees—I had the handle of a chaise riding-whip in my hand, and struck him over the head with it.
JAMES FROST ( police-constable L 59.) I took Mrs. Truebody on the 6th of Aug.—I was present on the evening after the riot was over—I took the females and the old gentleman into the house to take care of them.
JESSE DAY ( police-constable L 168.) John Hart was given into my cm tody—Elizabeth Hart and several others tackled Larkin again, and I went to assist him.
JOSEPH PRICE ( police-constable L 124.) I took Elizabeth Hart about one o'clock in the morning, on the 6th of Aug.
Finn's Defence. The old gentleman has been speaking very false to say I kicked him; I own I struck Larkin.
John Hart's Defence. My wife came and told me that Mr. Larkin had been threatening to hit her with a big stick; I went up to see what it was about, and there was the policeman and several others; I asked Larkin what be meant by it; he said he would give me in charge; Finn came up, and a quarrel took place; I went to get them away, old Larkin came up, with a big stick, and I shoved him away; his son came and laid hold of me, and held me by, the handkerchief for a quarter of an hour; and I held him; we weie fighting a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes; I asked him to let me go; I took my knife out and cut my neckcloth; I was never near Parsons.
FINN — GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
JOHN HART — GUILTY .— Confined Twelve Months.
ELIZABETH HART — GUILTY .— Confined Two Months.
TRUEBODY — GUILTY .— Confined One Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 21 ST OF SEPTEMBER.
